Presentation of some elementary properties of Segal-Bargmann space and of unitary Segal-Bargmann transform
with applications

Abstract




Abstract

In this work, we present some elementary properties of Segal-Bargmann space and some properties of unitary Segal Bargmann transform with applications to differential operators arising out of diffusion problem or of reggeon field theory.

1
Introduction

\rhd
We define Segal-Bargmann space
[4]
by:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{B}=\{\varphi:\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}\quad entire;\int_{\mathbb{C}}|\varphi(z)|^{2}e^{-|z|^{2}}dxdy<\infty.\}}

(1.1)

with the classical convention, where the inner product is linear in the first and anti-linear in second argument :

\displaystyle{<\varphi,\psi>=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\varphi(z)\overline{\psi(z)}e^{-|z|^{2}}dxdy;z=x+iy,(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}

(1.2)

and the associated norm is denoted by
\mid\mid.\mid\mid_{\mathbb{B}}
or simply by
\mid\mid.\mid\mid
:

\displaystyle{||\varphi||=\sqrt{\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}|\varphi(z)|^{2}e^{-|z|^{2}}dxdy}}

(1.3)

and

\rhd
We define
\displaystyle{L_{2}(\mathbb{C},d\mu(z))}
-space by:

\displaystyle{L_{2}(\mathbb{C},d\mu(z))=\{\varphi:\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}\quad mesearable;\int_{\mathbb{C}}|\varphi(z)|^{2}e^{-|z|^{2}}dxdy<\infty.\}}

(1.4)

where
\displaystyle{d\mu(z)=e^{-|z|^{2}}dxdy}
and with scalar product :

\displaystyle{<\varphi,\psi>=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\varphi(z)\overline{\psi(z)}e^{-|z|^{2}}dxdy;z=x+iy,(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}

(1.5)

The associated norm of
L_{2}(\mathbb{C},d\mu(z))
is denoted by
\mid\mid.\mid\mid_{L_{2}(\mathbb{C},d\mu(z))}

This space admits the following Hilbertian orthogonal decomposition:

\displaystyle{L_{2}(\mathbb{C},d\mu(z))=\underset{m\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}{\bigoplus}E_{m}(\mathbb{C})}

(1.6)

where the Hilbert subspaces
E_{m}(\mathbb{C}),m\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
are defined by

\displaystyle{E_{m}(\mathbb{C})=\{\varphi\in L_{2}(\mathbb{C},d\mu(z));\Lambda^{*}\Lambda\varphi=m\varphi\}}

(1.7)

In (1.7) the operators
\Lambda^{*}
and
\Lambda
are given respectively by
\displaystyle{\Lambda^{*}=-\frac{\partial}{\partial z}+\overline{z}}
and
\displaystyle{\Lambda=\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{z}}}
so that

\displaystyle{\Lambda^{*}\Lambda=-\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z\partial\overline{z}}+\overline{z}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}}

(1.8)

is a second order elliptic differential operator of Laplacian type and

\displaystyle{E_{0}(\mathbb{C})=\mathbb{B}}

(1.9)

i.e
\mathbb{B}
is the kernel of
\Lambda^{*}\Lambda
.

In the second section, We will show that

\bullet
Segal-Bargmann space
\mathbb{B}
is closed in
\displaystyle{L_{2}(\mathbb{C},d\mu(z))}
, and becomes a Hilbert space.

\bullet
Segal-Bargmann space
\mathbb{B}
is a reproducing kernel Hilbert space and it is determined by its reproducing kernel.

and

\rhd
we give some examples of classical reproducing kernel Hilbert space with their explicit kernels respectively.

\rhd
we show also that two reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces with the same kernel are equal.

In section 3, we define the Segal-Bargmann transform
S\mathcal{B}
as transformation of
L_{2}(\mathbb{R})
into
\displaystyle{L_{2}(\mathbb{C},d\mu(z))}
.

\rhd
if we denote the range of Segal-Bargmann transform by
SB(L_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
then we define an inner product:

\displaystyle{<\varphi,\psi>_{SB}=<f,g>_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R})}}

(1.10)

where
f
and
g
are chosen such that
SB(f)=\varphi
and
SB(g)=\psi
.

We show that
SB(L_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
is a reproducing kernel Hilbert space and that the Segal-Bargmann transform is injective, therfore it is an unitary mapping from
L_{2}(\mathbb{R})
to
SB(L_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
.

\rhd
we show that
SB(L_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
and
\mathbb{B}
have same kernel. This implies that
SB
is an unitary transform from
L_{2}(\mathbb{R})
to Segal-Bargmann space
\mathbb{B}
.

\rhd
We use knowledge basics of holomorphic functions of one complex variable and the properties of Hilbert spaces. In this work the knowledge of spectral theory and the functional analysis of standard level is required

2
The standard creation and annihilation operators on
L_{2}(\mathbb{R})

2.1
Basic properties of standard creation and annihilation operators on
L_{2}(\mathbb{R})

Definition 2.1
.

Let
(\mathcal{H}_{1},<,>_{1})
,
(\mathcal{H}_{2},<,>_{2})
, be two Hilbert spaces, consider an operator
T
from
\mathcal{H}_{1}
to
\mathcal{H}_{2}
and an operator
S
from
\mathcal{H}_{1}^{*}=\mathcal{H}_{1}
to
\mathcal{H}_{2}^{*}=\mathcal{H}_{2}
,
T
of domain
D(T)=\{u\in\mathcal{H}_{1},Tu\in\mathcal{H}_{2}\}
and
S
of domain
D(S)=\{v\in\mathcal{H}_{2}^{*},Sv\in\mathcal{H}_{1}^{*}\}
are said to be adjoint to each other if

\displaystyle{<Tu,v>=<u,Sv>,u\in D(T),v\in D(S)}

(2.1)

For each operator
T
from
\mathcal{H}_{1}
to
\mathcal{H}_{2}
, there are in general many operators
\mathcal{H}_{1}^{*}=\mathcal{H}_{1}
to
\mathcal{H}_{2}^{*}=\mathcal{H}_{2}
that are adjoint to
T
.

Definition 2.2
.

If
T
is densely defined (D(T) is dense in
\mathcal{H}_{1}
, however, there is a unique maximal operator
T^{*}
adjoint to
T
. This means that
T^{*}
is adjoint to
T
while any other
S
adjoint to
T
is a restriction of
T^{*}
.

T^{*}
is called the adjoint of
T
.

Remark 2.3
.

\bullet_{1}

T^{*}
is constructed in the following way .
D(T^{*})
consists of all
v\in\mathcal{H}_{2}^{*}
such that there exists an
v^{*}\in\mathcal{H}_{1}^{*}
with the property

\displaystyle{<Tu,v>=<u,v^{*}>\,\,\mbox{for all u}\,\,\in D(T)}

(2.2)

\bullet_{2}
the
v^{*}
is determined uniquely by
v
, for
u,v^{*}>=<u,v^{{}^{{}^{\prime}}}>
for all
u\in D(T)
implies
v^{*}=v^{{}^{{}^{\prime}}}
because
D(T)
is dense in
\mathcal{H}_{1}
by assumption.

\bullet_{3}
Therefore, an operator
T^{*}
from
\mathcal{H}_{2}^{*}
to
\mathcal{H}_{1}^{*}
is defined by setting
T^{*}v=v^{*}
. Obviously
T^{*}
is a linear operator, and comparison of
(2.1)
with
(2.3)
shows that
S\subset T^{*}
holds for any
S
adjoint to
T
while
T^{*}
itself is adjopint to
T.

\bullet_{3}
The adjointness relation
(2.1)
admits a simple interpretation in form of the graphs ( the graph
\displaystyle{\Gamma(T)}
of an operator
T
from
\mathcal{H}_{1}
to
\mathcal{H}_{2}
is by definition the subset of
\mathcal{H}_{1}\times\mathcal{H}_{2}
consisting of all elements of the form
\{u,Tu\}
with
u\in D(T)
).

The equation
(2.1)
can be written
\displaystyle{<u,-Sv>+<Tu,v>=0}
which that
T
and
S
are adjoint to each other if and only if the graph of
T
and the inverse graph of
-S
annihilate each other
\Gamma(T)=\Gamma^{{}^{\prime}}(-S)
.

Similarly
(2.2)
shows that the inverse graph of
-T^{*}
is the annihilator of the graph of
T
:

\Gamma^{{}^{\prime}}(-T^{*})=\Gamma(T)^{\perp}

(2.3)

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space equipped with scalar product
<,>
and let
T
be densely defined linear operator on
\mathcal{H}
.

Proposition 2.4
.

Let us define
\displaystyle{\mathbb{J}:\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H},(u,v)\longrightarrow(-v,u)}
. We equip
\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}
with the natural scalar product. If
T
is an operator with
dense domain

D(T)
, then

(i)
\displaystyle{\Gamma(T^{*})=\mathbb{J}(\Gamma(T))^{\perp}}
where
T^{*}
denotes its adjoint and
\Gamma(T)
is the graph of
T
and
\Gamma(T))^{\perp}
is the orthogonal of this graph.

(ii)
\displaystyle{\overline{\Gamma(T)}=\mathbb{J}(\Gamma(T^{*}))^{\perp}}
, where
\overline{\Gamma(T)}
denotes the closure of
\Gamma(T)

(iii) In particular,
T^{*}
is closed.

(iv)
T
is
closable
if and only if
D(T^{*})
is
dense
. In this case,
(T^{*})^{*}=T^{**}=\overline{T}
, where
\overline{T}
denotes the closure of
T
.

Proof

(i), (ii) and (iii):

Since
D(T)
is dense, we can assert that

\displaystyle{\Gamma(T^{*})=\{(u,v);v-T^{*}u=0\}}

\displaystyle{=\{(u,v);<v-T^{*}u,w>=0,\,\forall\,w\in D(T)\}}

\displaystyle{=\{(u,v);<v,w>-<u,Tw>=0,\,\forall\,w\in D(T)\}}

\displaystyle{=\{(u,v);<(u,v),\mathbb{J}(w,Tw)=0,\,\forall\,w\in D(T)\}=\mathbb{J}(\Gamma(T))^{\perp}}

It follows that

\displaystyle{\Gamma(T^{*})^{\perp}=(\mathbb{J}(\Gamma(T))^{\perp})^{\perp}=\overline{\mathbb{J}(\Gamma(T))}=\mathbb{J}(\overline{\Gamma(T)})}
.

Since
\mathbb{J}o\mathbb{J}=-\mathbb{I}
, we have

\displaystyle{\overline{\Gamma(A)}=-\mathbb{J}(\Gamma(T^{*})^{\perp})=\mathbb{J}(\Gamma(T^{*})^{\perp})=\mathbb{J}(\Gamma(T^{*}))^{\perp}}
.

The set
\Gamma(T^{*})
is closed as the orthogonal of the set
\mathbb{J}(\Gamma(T))
.

(iv) Assume that
D(T^{*})
is
dense
. Then, we have

\displaystyle{\Gamma((T^{*})^{*})=\mathbb{J}(\Gamma(T)^{*})^{\perp}=\mathbb{J}(\mathbb{J}(\Gamma(T)^{\perp})^{\perp}=\overline{\Gamma(T)}=\overline{\Gamma((T^{*})^{*})}}
.

In other words
\displaystyle{(T^{*})^{*}=T^{**}}
is closed with graph
\overline{\Gamma(T)}
. It follows that
T
is closable, and more precisely
\overline{T}=T^{**}
.

Now, assume that
T
is closable. Select
v\in D(T^{*})^{\perp}
. We have

\displaystyle{\forall\,w\in D(T^{*}),0=<v,w>=<(0,v),(-T^{*}w,w)>}
which means that

\displaystyle{(0,v)\in\mathbb{J}(\Gamma(T)^{*})^{\perp}=\overline{\Gamma(T)}=\Gamma(\overline{T})}
and therefore
v=T0=0
. By this way, we can recover
\displaystyle{(D(T^{*})^{\perp})^{\perp}=\{0\}^{\perp}=\mathcal{H}=\overline{D(T^{*})}}
as expected.
\blacksquare

Definition 2.5
.

(
Core of a linear operator
)

Let
T
be a closed operator in Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
with domain
D(T)
. A linear submanifold
D
of
D(T)
is called a
core
of
T
if the set of elements

\{\varphi,T\varphi\}
with
\varphi\in

D
is
dense
in
\Gamma(T)
(where
\Gamma(T)
is the graph of
T
). For this it is necessary (but not sufficient in general) that
D
is dense in
D(T)
.

The following theorem is due to von Neumann :

Theorem 2.6
.

(
von Neumann
)

Let
\mathcal{H}_{1}
,
\mathcal{H}_{2}
be Hilbert space. Let
\displaystyle{T:\mathcal{H}_{1}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{2},\varphi\longrightarrow T\varphi}
be a closed and densely defined operator Then

(i)
T^{*}T
is a
self-adjoint
operator in
\mathcal{H}_{1}

and

(ii)
D(T^{*}T)
is a
core
of
T
.

Proof

(see
[22]
p. 275)

From eq.
(2.3)
and (i) of proposition
(2.4)
, It is known that the graphs
\Gamma(T)
and
\Gamma^{{}^{\prime}}(-T^{*})
are complementary subspaces of the Hilbert space
\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}_{1}\times\mathcal{H}_{2}}
. This implies that any vector
\displaystyle{(u,u^{\prime})\in\mathcal{H}_{1}\times\mathcal{H}_{2}}
, there exist a unique
v\in D(T)
and
v^{\prime}\in D(T^{*})
such that it can be expressed in the form

\displaystyle{(u,u^{\prime})=(-Tv,v)+(v^{\prime},T^{*}v^{\prime})}
with
v\in D(T)
and
v^{\prime}\in D(T^{*})
.
(
\star_{1}
)

That is

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}u=v^{\prime}-Tv\\
\\
u^{\prime}=v+T^{*}v^{\prime}\\
\end{array}\right.

(
\star_{2}
)

Furthermore, taking the
\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}_{1}\times\mathcal{H}_{2}norm}
in
(
\star_{1}
)
gives

\displaystyle{||u||^{2}+||u^{\prime}||^{2}=||v||^{2}+||Tv||^{2}+||v^{\prime}||^{2}+||T^{*}v^{\prime}||^{2}}
.
(
\star_{3}
)

Applying
(
\star_{2}
)
to
u=0
shows that for every
u^{\prime}
there exists a
v\in D(T)
and a
v^{\prime}\in D(T^{*}
such that

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}v^{\prime}=Tv\\
\\
u^{\prime}=v+T^{*}v^{\prime}\\
\end{array}\right.

(
\star_{4}
)

This is to say that
v\in D(T^{*}T)
and
u^{\prime}=(I+T^{*}T)v

The relation (
(
\star_{3}
)
gives
\displaystyle{||v||\leq||u^{\prime}||}
in this case, because
||u||=0
.

Hence the operator
I+T^{*}T
is injective and as since
u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}
was arbitrary, it follows that
\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}=I+T^{*}T}
has range
\mathcal{H}
so
\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}^{-1}=(I+T^{*}T)^{-1}}
is a bounded operator.

Now for all
u^{\prime},u^{\prime\prime}\in H_{1}
we have, for some
u_{1},u_{2}\in D(T^{*}T)

\displaystyle{<(I+T^{*}T)^{-1}u^{\prime},u^{\prime\prime}>=<u_{1}+T^{*}Tu_{1},u_{2}>=<u_{1},u_{2}>+<Tu_{1},Tu_{2}>}

(
\star_{5}
)

In particular
I+T^{*}T)^{-1}
is self-adjoint, hence
I+T^{*}T
and
T^{*}T
are self-adjoint.

(ii) If
w\in D(T)
is orthogonal to every
v\in D(T^{*}T)
for the scalar product
<,>_{T}
then, for all
v\in D(T^{*}T)
we have

\displaystyle{0=<v,w>_{T}:=<v,w>+<Tv,Tw>=<v,w>+<T^{*}Tv,w>}

(
\star_{5}
)

That is,
w
belongs to
\mathcal{I}m(I+T^{*}T)^{\perp}
(where
\mathcal{I}m(I+T^{*}T)
denotes the range of
I+T^{*}T
) , which is
\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\perp}
because
I+T^{*}T
is surjective as was noticed above. Hence
w=0
and the density is proved.
\blacksquare

\rhd
Now, we discuss here two important operators. Take
\mathcal{H}=L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
(Here
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
is the Hilbert space of square-integrable functions on
\mathbb{R}
).

Let us introduce the following differential operators, acting on

\displaystyle{D(A)=D(B)=\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}),A:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\frac{d}{dx}+x)\,and\,B:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(-\frac{d}{dx}+x)}
, where:.

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})=\{f:\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C};f\in C_{0}^{k}(\mathbb{R}),\,\,\mbox{for all}\,\ k\,\,\mbox{and}\,\,l\in\mathbb{N}\}}
such that

\displaystyle{<x>^{-l}C_{0}^{k}(\mathbb{R})=\{f\in C_{0}^{k}(\mathbb{R});f=<x>^{-l}g;g\in C_{0}^{k}(\mathbb{R})\,\,\mbox{for all}\,\ k\,\ \mbox{and}\,\ l\in\mathbb{N}\}}

with norm,
\displaystyle{||f||_{k,l}=||g||_{C^{k}},g=<x>^{l}f}
and
\displaystyle{<x>=\sqrt{1+|x|^{2}}}
a function which is the size of
|x|
for
|x|
large, but has the virtue of being smooth.

The operators
B
and
A
are called
Creation
and
annihilation
operators.

We observe that the domains of their adjoints are

\displaystyle{D(A^{*})=\{\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R});(-\frac{d}{dx}+x)\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\}}
with
\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})\subset D(A^{*})}

and

\displaystyle{D(B^{*})=\{\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R});(\frac{d}{dx}+x)\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\}}
with
\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})\subset D(B^{*})}
.

It follows that
D(A^{*})
and
D(B^{*})
are dense in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

By the above property (iv), the two operators
A
and
B
are
closable
with
\overline{A}=A^{**}
and
\overline{B}=B^{**}
. On the other hand

\bullet

\displaystyle{\forall\,\psi\in D(A^{*}),A^{*}\psi=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(-\frac{d}{dx}+x)\psi=B\psi}
.

\bullet

\displaystyle{\forall\,\psi\in D(B^{*}),B^{*}\psi=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\frac{d}{dx}+x)\psi=A\psi}
.

In other words, we have
A\subset B^{*}
and
B\subset A^{*}
, whereas (by direct computations) we can check that
\Gamma(A^{*})
and
\Gamma(B^{*})
are closed, so that
\overline{A}\subset B^{*}
and
\overline{B}\subset A^{*}
.

Proposition 2.7
.

Let
\overline{A}
and
\overline{B}
be the colosure of
A
and
B
respectively, then we have

(i)

\displaystyle{D(\overline{A})=D(\overline{B})={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathbb{D}}=\{\psi\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R});x\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\}}
.

(ii)

\overline{A}
and
\overline{B}
are
adjoints of one another
and they share the same domain

\mathbb{D}

Proof

For all
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
, we have

(i)
\displaystyle{2\mid\mid A\varphi\mid\mid^{2}=\mid\mid\varphi^{\prime}\mid\mid^{2}+\mid\mid x\varphi\mid\mid^{2}-\mid\mid\varphi\mid\mid^{2}}
.

(ii)
\displaystyle{2\mid\mid B\varphi\mid\mid^{2}=\mid\mid\varphi^{\prime}\mid\mid^{2}+\mid\mid x\varphi\mid\mid^{2}+\mid\mid\varphi\mid\mid^{2}}
.

Now, take
\varphi\in D(\overline{A})
. By definition, we have
\displaystyle{(\varphi,\overline{A}\varphi)\in\overline{\Gamma(A)}}
. There exists
\varphi_{n}\in D(A)
such that
(\varphi_{n})
converges to
\varphi
and
(A\varphi_{n})
converges to
\overline{A}u
. We deduce that
(\varphi_{n}^{{}^{\prime}})
and
(x\varphi_{n})
are Cauchy sequences in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. By this way, we get that
(\varphi_{n}^{{}^{\prime}})\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
(x\varphi_{n})\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, and therefore

D(\overline{A})\subset{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathbb{D}}
. We can proceed in the same way for
\overline{B}
.

We deal now with the reversed inclusion. Take
\varphi\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathbb{D}}
. By using a classical result :

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}
is
dense
in
({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathbb{D},\mid\mid.\mid\mid_{\mathbb{D}}})
, there exists a sequence
(\varphi_{n})
of
smooth
functions with
compact support
such that
\varphi_{n}
converges to
\varphi
in
\mathbb{D}
. In particular
(A\varphi_{n})
and
(B\varphi_{n})
converge in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, so that
(\varphi,A\varphi)\in\overline{\Gamma(A)}=\Gamma(A)
as well as

(\varphi,B\varphi)\in\overline{\Gamma(B)}=\Gamma(B)
. This implies
\varphi\in D(\overline{A})
and
\varphi\in D(\overline{B})
.

Now to prove that the closures
\overline{A}
and
\overline{B}
of
A
and
B
are
adjoints of one another
and they share the same domain

\mathbb{D}
, we consider :

\displaystyle{\mathcal{V}_{\pm}=\{\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R});(\pm\frac{d}{dx}+x)\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\}\subset L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}
.

We let, for all
\displaystyle{\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{V}_{\pm}}
,

\displaystyle{Q_{\pm}(\varphi,\psi)=<\varphi,\psi>_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}+<(\pm\frac{d}{dx}+x)\varphi,(\pm\frac{d}{dx}+x)\psi>_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}}
.

Then we have

(
\alpha
)
\displaystyle{(\mathcal{V}_{\pm},Q_{\pm})}
is a Hilbert space.

(
\beta
) For
\displaystyle{f\in\mathcal{V}_{\pm}}
the sequence
\displaystyle{f_{n}=\chi_{n}(\rho_{n}\star f)}
converges in
\mathcal{V}_{\pm}
.

where
(\rho_{n})
is a sequence of smooth non-negative functions such that
\displaystyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}}\rho_{n}(x)dx=1}
with
supp\rho_{n}=B(0,\frac{1}{n})
and
\chi_{n}(.)=\chi(\frac{1}{n})
with
\chi
is a smooth function with compact support
0\leq\chi\leq 1
equal to
1
in a neighborhood of
0
.

For example, if
\psi\in D(B^{*})
, we have
\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
\displaystyle{(\frac{d}{dx}+x)\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}
. There exists
\psi_{n}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
such that
\psi_{n}
converges to
\psi
and
\displaystyle{(\frac{d}{dx}+x)\psi_{n}}
converges to
\displaystyle{(\frac{d}{dx}+x)\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}
. Using (ii) we get that
(\psi_{n})
and
(x\psi_{n})
are Cauchy sequences in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
with limit
\psi^{\prime}
and
x\psi
. Thus
\psi\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathbb{D}}
.

From the inclusion
\overline{A}\subset B^{*}
, we deduce that

\displaystyle{D(B^{*})\subset{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathbb{D}}=D(\overline{A})\subset D(B^{*})}
.

We can deal with
A
in the same way to obtain

\displaystyle{D(B^{*})=D(\overline{A})={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathbb{D}}=D(\overline{B})\subset D(A^{*})}
.

We deduce that

\bullet

\displaystyle{\overline{A}=B^{*},\overline{B}=A^{*}}

\bullet

\displaystyle{\overline{A}^{*}=A^{*}=\overline{B},\overline{B}^{*}=B^{*}=\overline{A}}
.
\blacksquare

2.2
Determination of eigenvalues and eigenfunctions of Hermite’s operator

Let
\mathcal{H}=L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
\displaystyle{\mathcal{V}=\{u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R});\sqrt{1+x^{2}}u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\}}

and

\displaystyle{a(u,v)=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}u^{\prime}(x)\overline{v^{\prime}(x)}dx+\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}(1+x^{2})u(x)\overline{v(x)}dx}
.

Then the operator associated to this form called Hermite’s operator is defined by:

Au=-u^{\prime\prime}+(1+x^{2})u
with domain
\displaystyle{D(A)=\{u\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R});(1+x^{2})u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\}}
.

Note that one clearly has
\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})\subset D(A)\subset H^{2}(\mathbb{R})}
, whence
A
is densely defined. It is also a closed operator. To see this, we have to show that

\displaystyle{(u_{j}\in D(A)\longrightarrow u\,and\,Au_{j}\longrightarrow v,as\,j\longrightarrow\infty)\Longrightarrow u\in D(A)\,and\,Au=v}

Since
\displaystyle{u_{j}\longrightarrow u\,(\,in\,L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))\,\Longrightarrow u_{j}\longrightarrow u\,(\,in\,\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}))\,as\,j\longrightarrow\infty}
.

we have on the one hand
\displaystyle{Au_{j}\longrightarrow Au=v\,(\,in\,\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}))\,as\,j\longrightarrow\infty}
.

Since
v\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
then
u\in D(H)
and
Au=v
, which is what we wanted to prove.

Now, we think of
A
as an unbounded operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
with dense domain
D(A)
. Then we have the following facts.

\bullet
The injection of
D(A)
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
is compact .

In fact, it suffices to use the theorems of compact embeddings of Sobolev spaces in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

\bullet
Self-adjointness:
A=A^{*}
.

\bullet

\displaystyle{\sigma(A)=\{2(n+1);n\in\mathbb{N}\}}
with multiplicity
1
.

\bullet
Define
\displaystyle{h_{0}=e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2}}}
and
\displaystyle{h_{n}=a_{+}^{n}(h_{0}),n\in\mathbb{N}}
where
\displaystyle{a_{+}=-\frac{d}{dx}+x}
. Then
\displaystyle{a_{-}(h_{0})=0}
where
\displaystyle{a_{-}=\frac{d}{dx}+x}
, all the functions
h_{n}
belong to
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
and are of the form
\displaystyle{h_{n}=H_{n}e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2}}}
, where
\displaystyle{H_{n}\in\mathbb{R}[X]}
is a polynomial of degree
n
(i.e., of the form
\displaystyle{a_{n}x^{n}+a_{n-1}x^{n-1}+....+a_{1}x+a_{0}}
, with non-zero leading coefficient
a_{n}
). The polynomials
H_{n}
are called
Hermite polynomials
. Moreover, the
h_{n}
form a complete orthogonal system of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

Hence, upon defining
\displaystyle{u_{n}=\frac{h_{n}}{\mid\mid h_{n}\mid\mid}}
, the system
\displaystyle{\{u_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}}
is an orthonormal
basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. The functions
h_{n}
are called
Hermite functions
.

We have, by a direct computation that
\displaystyle{a_{+}a_{-}=A-2I}
,
\displaystyle{a_{+}a_{-}=A}
and
\displaystyle{[a_{+},a_{-}]=-2I}

The operators
a_{\pm}
are the celebrated
creation
(
a_{+}
) and
annihilation
(
a_{-}
) operators which are adjoint of aech other (see the above subsection)

Note, in the first place, that for any given
u_{1},u_{2}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
we have

\displaystyle{<Au_{1},u_{2}>=<u_{1},Au_{2}>}
(i.e.
A
is symmetric).

and

\displaystyle{<Au,u>=<(a_{+}a_{-}+2I)u,u>=\mid\mid a_{-}u\mid\mid^{2}+2\mid\mid u\mid\mid^{2},\,\forall\,u\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})}
.

On the other hand, since
a_{-}h_{0}=0
and
h_{0}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
, we indeed have that

\displaystyle{min_{u\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})-\{0\}}\frac{<Au,u>}{\mid\mid u\mid\mid^{2}}}
.

Next, it is clear that the functions
\displaystyle{h_{n}=a_{+}^{n}h_{0}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})\,\forall\,n\in\mathbb{N}}
. Hence we may
compute by induction :

\displaystyle{Ah_{0}=2h_{0}}

\displaystyle{Ah_{n}=(a_{+}a_{-}+2I)u_{n}=(a_{+}a_{-}+2I)a_{+}h_{n-1}=a_{+}Ah_{n-1}+2a_{+}h_{n-1}}

\displaystyle{=(\lambda_{n-1}+2)h_{n}=\lambda_{n}h_{n}}
.

This implies
\displaystyle{\lambda_{n}=\lambda_{n-1}+2}
with
\lambda_{0}=2
, it follows that
\displaystyle{Ah_{n}=2(n+1)h_{n}}
.

Of course, we have to make sure that
h_{n}\neq 0
for all
n
. Hence, we compute by induction:

\displaystyle{\mid\mid h_{n}\mid\mid^{2}=<h_{n},h_{n}>=<a_{+}^{n}h_{0},a_{+}^{n}h_{0}>=<a_{-}^{n}a_{+}^{n}h_{0},h_{0}>}

\displaystyle{=<a_{-}^{n-1}[a_{-}a_{+}]a_{+}^{n-1}h_{0},h_{0}>}

\displaystyle{=2(n-1)<a_{-}^{n-1}a_{+}^{n-1}h_{0},h_{0}>=2(n-1)<a_{+}^{n-1}h_{0},a_{+}^{n-1}h_{0}>}

\displaystyle{=2(n-1)\mid\mid h_{n-1}\mid\mid^{2}=2^{n}(n-1)!\mid\mid h_{0}\mid\mid^{2}}
.

whence
h_{n}\neq 0
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Now, since
a_{-}h_{0}=0
a similar computation also shows that

\displaystyle{<h_{n},h_{m}>=0}
,

that yields the orthogonality of the system
\{h_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. To prove its completeness, we note in the first place that it is clear that
h_{n}=H_{n}h_{0}
, for a real polynomial
H_{n}
of degree
n
(i.e.,
H_{n}(x)=a_{n}x^{n}+a_{n-1}x^{n-1}+...+a_{0}
, with the
a_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
and non-zero leading coefficient
a_{n}
).

Hence, let
g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
be such that
\displaystyle{\int_{\infty}^{+\infty}g(x)h_{n}(x)dx=0\,\forall\,n\in\mathbb{N}}
.

It then follows that, since any polynomial
P\in\mathcal{R}[x]
can be written as a linear combination of the
H_{n}
(by virtue of the fact that their leading coefficients are not zero), we have

\displaystyle{\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}g(x)P(x)e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2}}dx=0\quad\forall\,P\in\mathbb{R}[X]}
.
(*)

Using
\displaystyle{e^{-ix\xi}=\sum_{j\geq 0}\frac{(-ix\xi)^{j}}{j!}}
and
(*)
to get

\displaystyle{\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}e^{-ix\xi}g(x)e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2}}(x)dx=\mathfrak{F}_{x\longrightarrow\xi}(ge^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2}})(\xi)=0,\,\forall\,\xi\in\mathbb{R}}
.

But an
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
-function which has zero Fourier-transform must be zero, that is

\displaystyle{ge^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2}}=0\Longrightarrow g=0\,in\,L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}
.

Hence
\{h_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is an orthogonal basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
\{u_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, where
\displaystyle{u_{n}:=\frac{h_{n}}{\mid\mid h_{n}\mid\mid}}
, is an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

Using this, one then sees that
\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}(H\pm iI)=L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}
.

whence, by general arguments see, for instance,
[11]
or
[22]

A=A^{*}
.

This therefore shows that
\displaystyle{\sigma(A)=\{2(n+1),n\in\mathbb{N}}
with multiplicity
1
, and concludes the determination of eigenvalues and eigenfunctions of Hermite’s operator.
\blacksquare

2.3
Elementary properties of Bargmann space

We denote
\displaystyle{L_{2}(\mathbb{C},d\mu(z))}
the Hilbert space of complex measurable functions
f(z,\overline{z})
on the complex plane
\mathbb{C}
, not necessarily analytical, which are square integrable with respect to the Gaussian measure

\displaystyle{d\mu(z)=\frac{1}{\pi}e^{-|z|^{2}}dzd\overline{z};z=x+iy,i^{2}=-1,(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}.

(2.4)

[being
dzd\overline{z}
the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{C}
and
\displaystyle{\overline{z}=x-iy),(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}
].

We recall that the Bargmann space
[4]
, denoted by

\displaystyle{\mathbb{B}=\{\varphi:\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}\,\,\mbox{ analytic}\,\,;\int_{\mathbb{C}}|\varphi(z)|^{2}e^{-|z|^{2}}dxdy<\infty\}}

(2.5)

is the subspace of all entire functions in
\displaystyle{L_{2}(\mathbb{C},d\mu(z))}
with the inner product (1.2)

Lemma 2.8
.

The Bargmann space
\mathbb{B}
is injected continuously into the space of distributions
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

Proof

Let
\varphi(x,y)\in\mathfrak{D}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
\psi(x,y)\in\mathfrak{D}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
where
\mathfrak{D}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is the space of indefinitely differentiable functions of compact support.

Then we have:

\displaystyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi(x,y)\bar{\psi}(x,y)dxdy}=\displaystyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{\frac{-(x^{2}+y^{2})}{2}}\phi(x,y)e^{\frac{x^{2}+y^{2}}{2}}\bar{\psi}(x,y)dxdy}.

and apply Schwarz’s inequality to obtain the following inequality :

\displaystyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi(x,y)\bar{\psi}(x,y)dxdy}\leq\mid\mid\phi\mid\mid_{E}\mid\mid e^{\frac{x^{2}+y^{2}}{2}}\psi\mid\mid_{L^{2}(I\!\!R^{2})}
.

From this inequality we deduce that
\mathbb{B}
is injected continuously into the space of distributions
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.
\blacksquare

Lemma 2.9
.

(i) Any Cauchy sequence in
\mathbb{B}
is also Cauchy in the space of continuous functions
\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{C})}
endowed with the topology of compact
convergence.

(ii) The Bargmann space is a Hilbert space.

Proof

(i) In this topology a sequence of functions
\{\varphi_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
converges if and only if for every compact set
K\subset\mathbb{C}
the sequence converges uniformly on
K
.

The goal is to find for any
K
as above an estimate of the form

\displaystyle{\underset{z\in K}{sup}|\varphi_{n}-\varphi_{m}|\leq C_{K}||\varphi_{n}-\varphi_{m}||_{B}}

(2.6)

where
C_{K}
is a constant depending on the compact set
K
. Once we have this, we find for each
K
a uniform bound and hence the sequence f
\{\varphi_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
is Cauchy in
\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{C})}
if it is Cauchy in
\mathbb{B}
.

Let
K\subset\mathbb{C}
be compact. To simplify some computations choose
r>0
such that
K
is contained in
B_{r}
, the ball with radius
r
around the origin. Then pick any smooth function with compact support
\displaystyle{\chi\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}}
such that
\displaystyle{\chi(z)=1}
for all
\displaystyle{z\in B_{r+1}}
. Here and in the following,
B_{r}
denotes the sphere of radius
r
.

Set
\displaystyle{U=supp\,\,\chi-B_{r+1}}
. This is arranged so that
U
is a compact set with distance one to
B_{r}
and
\chi
vanishes on all parts of the boundary of
U
except on
\partial B_{r+1}
, where it is one. Since
K\subset B_{r}
we have

\displaystyle{\underset{z\in K}{sup}|\varphi_{n}-\varphi_{m}|\leq\underset{z\in B_{r}}{sup}|\varphi_{n}-\varphi_{m}|}

(2.7)

By the Bochner-Martinelli generalization of Cauchy’s integral theorem and Stokes? theorem, we find that the norm in Lebesgue measure
dz

is equivalent to the norm in Gauss measure
\displaystyle{d\mu(z)=e^{-|z|^{2}}dz}
. Thus

\displaystyle{||\varphi_{n}-\varphi_{m}||_{L_{2}(U)}\leq c||\varphi_{n}-\varphi_{m}||_{L_{2}(U,d\mu)}\leq c||\varphi_{n}-\varphi_{m}||_{\mathbb{B}}}

(2.8)

for some constant c depending on
U
.

Putting all this together we arrive at the desired estimate (2.6). Notice that the constant depends on
r
and on
U
, but it is readily seen that
r
and
U
depend on
K
only.

(ii) Let
\{\varphi_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
be a Cauchy sequence in
\mathbb{B}
. Then by the above result it is a Cauchy sequence in
\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{C})}
with the topology of compact convergence. Since this space is complete, there exists a limit function
\displaystyle{\varphi\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{C})}
. By a theorem of Weierstrass (see for example
[24], Ch.V]
) this function is holomorphic.

The sequence
\{\varphi_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
is Cauchy in
\mathbb{B}
, so that the
||\varphi_{n}||_{\mathbb{B}}
are bounded by a constant
C
. From the uniform convergence on compacta it follows that for any compact set
K\subset\mathbb{C}

\displaystyle{\int_{K}|\varphi(z)|^{2}d\mu(z)=\lim\limits_{n\longrightarrow+\infty}\int_{K}|\varphi_{n}(z)|^{2}d\mu(z)\leq||\varphi_{n}||_{\mathbb{B}}^{2}\leq C^{2}}

(2.9)

and we see that
\varphi
is square integrable on every compact subset. By monotone convergence we find that
||\varphi||\leq C
, so
\varphi\in\mathbb{B}
.. Using the triangle inequality we see that
\displaystyle{||\varphi-\varphi_{n}||_{\mathbb{B}}\leq 2C\,\,\mbox{for}n=1,2,...}
. Thus we may apply Fubini theorem to obtain

\begin{array}[]{c}\displaystyle{\lim\limits_{n\longrightarrow+\infty}||\varphi-\varphi_{n}||_{\mathbb{B}}=\lim\limits_{n\longrightarrow+\infty}\underset{K}{sup}\int_{K}|\varphi(z)-\varphi_{n}(z)|^{2}d\mu(z)}\\
\\
\displaystyle{=\underset{K}{sup}\int_{K}\lim\limits_{n\longrightarrow+\infty}|\varphi(z)-\varphi_{n}(z)|^{2}d\mu(z)}\end{array}

(2.10)

which is zero because the
\varphi_{n}
converge uniformly to
\varphi
on each compact set
K
. We conclude that

\displaystyle{||\varphi-\varphi_{n}||_{\mathbb{B}}\longrightarrow 0\,\,\mbox{as}\,\,n\longrightarrow+\infty,\,\,\mbox{so}\,\,\varphi_{n}\longrightarrow\varphi\,\,\mbox{in}\,\,\mathbb{B}}

(2.11)

This completes the proof.
\blacksquare

Definition 2.10
.

Recall that a linear operator
T
on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
with domain
\displaystyle{D(T)\subset\mathcal{H}}
is said to be well defined
if its domain is dense in this space.

As a holomorphic function
\varphi
can be expressed as a power series:

\displaystyle{\varphi(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}z^{n}}

(2.12)

We will show that it is possible to rewrite the inner product on
\mathbb{B}
in terms of the expansion coefficients
a_{n}
.

To do this we will calculate (1.2) for
\psi=\varphi
using polar coordinates
z=re^{i\theta}
;
r\in[0,+\infty[
et
\theta\in[0,2\pi]
and the power series expression of
\varphi
. We then get

Lemma 2.11
.

\displaystyle{||\varphi||_{\mathbb{B}}^{2}=\pi\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}n!|a_{n}|^{2}}

(2.13)

Proof

Let
\varphi\in\mathbb{B}
and if we put
z=re^{i\theta}
;
r\in[0,+\infty[
and
\theta\in[0,2\pi]
then

\displaystyle{\varphi(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}z^{n}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}r^{n}e^{in\theta}}
and we can write:

\displaystyle{||\varphi||_{\mathbb{B}}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{C}}e^{-\mid z\mid^{2}}\mid\varphi(z)\mid^{2}dzd\bar{z}}=\displaystyle{\lim\limits_{\sigma\longrightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{\sigma}\int_{0}^{2\pi}e^{-r^{2}}\mid\phi(re^{i\theta})\mid^{2}rdrd\theta}

and

\mid\varphi(re^{i\theta})\mid^{2}=\displaystyle{\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}\displaystyle{\sum_{p+q=n}a_{p}\bar{a}_{q}r^{p+q}e^{i(p-q)\theta}}}

By Beppo-Levi and Fubini theorems, we obtain that:

\displaystyle{\int_{\mathbb{C}}e^{-\mid z\mid^{2}}\mid\varphi(z)\mid^{2}dzd\bar{z}}=\displaystyle{\lim\limits_{\sigma\longrightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{\sigma}e^{-r^{2}}}\displaystyle{\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}}\displaystyle{\sum_{p+q=n}a_{p}\bar{a}_{q}r^{p+q+1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}e^{i(p-q)\theta}drd\theta}

As
\displaystyle{\int_{0}^{2\pi}e^{i(p-q)\theta}d\theta}=2\pi\delta_{pq}
where
\delta_{pq}
is Kronoecker’s symbol then we get:

\displaystyle{\int_{\mathbb{C}}e^{-\mid z\mid^{2}}\mid\varphi(z)\mid^{2}dzd\bar{z}}=\displaystyle{\lim\limits_{\sigma\longrightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{\sigma}e^{-r^{2}}}\displaystyle{\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}}\displaystyle{\sum_{2p=n}a_{p}\bar{a}_{p}r^{2p+1}2\pi dr}

=2\pi\displaystyle{\lim\limits_{\sigma\longrightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{\sigma}e^{-r^{2}}}\displaystyle{\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}a_{k}\bar{a}_{k}r^{2k+1}dr}

Again by applying Beppo-Levi’s theorem, we obtain:

\displaystyle{\int_{\mathbb{C}}e^{-\mid z\mid^{2}}\mid\varphi(z)\mid^{2}dzd\bar{z}}=\displaystyle{\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}\mid a_{k}\mid^{2}}\displaystyle{\lim\limits_{\sigma\longrightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{\sigma}e^{-r^{2}}r^{2k+1}2\pi dr}

But

\displaystyle{\lim\limits_{\sigma\longrightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{\sigma}e^{-r^{2}}r^{2k+1}2\pi dr=\pi k!}

Therefore:

\displaystyle{\int_{\mathbb{C}}e^{-\mid z\mid^{2}}\mid\phi(z)\mid^{2}dzd\bar{z}}=\pi\displaystyle{\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}k!\mid a_{k}\mid^{2}<+\infty}

from which we conclude that the condition for a function
\varphi
to be an element of
\mathbb{B}
is given by

\displaystyle{\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}n!|a_{n}|^{2}<\infty}

(2.14)

Using the linearity of the inner product:

\displaystyle{<\varphi+\psi,\varphi+\psi>_{\mathbb{B}}=<\varphi,\varphi>_{\mathbb{B}}+<\varphi,\psi>_{\mathbb{B}}+<\psi,\varphi>_{\mathbb{B}}+<\psi,\psi>_{\mathbb{B}}}

For
\displaystyle{\varphi(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}z^{n}}
and
\displaystyle{\psi(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}b_{n}z^{n}}
, we also find

\noindent\displaystyle{<\varphi,\psi>_{\mathbb{B}}=\pi\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}n!a_{n}\overline{b_{n}}}

(2.15)

\blacksquare

We also find

Lemma 2.12
.

(Complete orthonormal system in
\mathbb{B}
)

Let
\displaystyle{e_{n}(z)=\frac{z^{n}}{\sqrt{n!}},n\in\mathbb{N}}
then

The system
\displaystyle{\{e_{n}(z);n\in\mathbb{N}\}}
forms a complete orthonormal system in
\mathbb{B}

Proof

\bullet

\displaystyle{<z^{n},z^{m}>=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}z^{n}\overline{z}^{m}e^{-|z|^{2}}dxdy}

\displaystyle{=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}e^{i(n-m)\theta}d\theta\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{n+m}e^{-r^{2}}2rdr}

\displaystyle{=\delta_{n,m}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{n}e^{-t}dt}

\displaystyle{=n!\delta_{n,m}}
.

In particular

\bullet

\displaystyle{<\frac{z^{n}}{\sqrt{n!}},\frac{z^{m}}{\sqrt{m!}}>=\delta_{n,m}}

This gives that the system
\displaystyle{\{e_{n}(z);n\in\mathbb{N}\}}
is orthonormal with the norm of each
e_{n}
given by
\displaystyle{||e_{n}||_{\mathbb{B}}=1}
.

In this computation the substitutions
z=re^{i\theta}
with
\theta\in[0,2\pi]
,
r\in[0,\infty[
and
t=r^{2}
have been made.

\bullet
Let
\varphi\in\mathbb{B}
. Then
\varphi
is holomorphic and we can write it as a power series
\displaystyle{\varphi(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}z^{n}}
, converging uniformly on compact subsets of
\mathbb{C}
.

\bullet
Suppose that
\displaystyle{<z^{m},\varphi>=0}
for all
m\in\mathbb{N}

Using first dominated convergence and then the uniform convergence on compact sets, we find that

\displaystyle{<z^{m},\varphi>=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}z^{m}\overline{\varphi(z)}e^{-|z|^{2}}dxdy}

\displaystyle{=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}z^{m}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\overline{a}_{n}\,\overline{z}^{n}dxdy}

\displaystyle{=\frac{1}{2\pi}\lim\limits_{r\longrightarrow+\infty}\int_{0}^{r}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\overline{a}_{n}e^{i(m-n)\theta}r^{m+n}e^{-r^{2}}2rdr}

\displaystyle{=\lim\limits_{r\longrightarrow+\infty}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\overline{a}_{n}\delta_{n,m}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{n}e^{-t}dt}

\displaystyle{=m!\overline{a}_{m}}
.

and hence
a_{m}=0
for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
, giving
\varphi=0
. This shows the completeness of the system
\displaystyle{\{e_{n}(z);n\in\mathbb{N}\}}
.
\blacksquare

Corollary 2.13
.

The set of polynomials
\mathcal{P}
is dense in
\mathbb{B}
.

Now we define :

Definition 2.14
.

\displaystyle{\mathbb{B}_{S}=\{(a_{n})_{n=0}^{\infty}\in\mathbb{C};\displaystyle{\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}n!|a_{n}|^{2}<\infty\}}}

(2.16)

equipped with inner product

\displaystyle{<(a_{n})_{n=0}^{\infty},(b_{n})_{n=0}^{\infty}>_{\mathbb{B}_{S}}=\pi\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}n!a_{n}\overline{b_{n}}}

(2.17)

Proposition 2.15
.

\mathbb{B}
is related to
\mathbb{B}_{S}
by an unitary transform of
\mathbb{B}
onto
\mathbb{B}_{S}
, i.e. an
isomorphism between
\mathbb{B}
and
\mathbb{B}_{S}
that preserves the inner product, given by the following transform
I
:

\displaystyle{I=\mathbb{B}\longrightarrow\mathbb{B}_{S},\varphi\longrightarrow I(\varphi)=(\frac{1}{n!}\varphi^{(n)}(0))_{n=0}^{\infty}=(a_{n})_{n=0}^{\infty}}

(2.18)

Proof

It follows from (2.9) that

\mid\mid\phi\mid\mid_{\mathbb{B}}=\mid\mid(\frac{1}{n!}\varphi^{(n)}(0))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mid\mid_{\mathbb{B}_{S}}\\

(2.19)

and from (2.14), we deduce that
I
is continuous and if
I(\varphi)=0
then
\varphi=0
, i.e.
I
is injective.

Now, as
I
is continuous and
\mathcal{R}^{\perp}(I)=\{0\}
where
\mathcal{R}(I)
denotes the range of
I
and
\mathcal{R}^{\perp}(I)
is its orthogonal, then
I
is a surjective map between
\mathbb{B}
and
\mathbb{B}_{S}
, it follows that
I
is an isomorphism.
\blacksquare

Proposition 2.16
.

(1) For all
\varphi\in\mathbb{B}
, we have the following inequalities:

(i)\mid\varphi(z)\mid\leq e^{\frac{\mid z\mid^{2}}{2}}\mid\mid\varphi\mid\mid_{\mathbb{B}},
for all
z\in\mathbb{C}

(ii)\mid\varphi^{{}^{\prime}}(z)\mid\leq(1+\mid z\mid^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{\frac{\mid z\mid^{2}}{2}}\mid\mid\varphi\mid\mid_{\mathbb{B}}
, for all
z\in\mathbb{C}

(iii)\mid\varphi^{{}^{\prime\prime}}(z)\mid\leq(\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1}\mid z\mid^{2}+\alpha_{2}\mid z\mid^{4})^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{\frac{\mid z\mid^{2}}{2}}\mid\mid\varphi\mid\mid_{\mathbb{B}}
, for all
z\in\mathbb{C}
and
\alpha_{i}>0,i=0,1,2
.

(2)
\mathbb{B}
is a closed subspace of
L_{2}(\mathbb{C},d\mu(z))
.

Proof

\bullet
(1)

(i) Let
\varphi\in\mathbb{B}
then by using Cauchy-Shwarz inequality we deduce that:

\displaystyle{\mid\varphi(z)\mid\leq\displaystyle{\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}\mid a_{k}z^{k}\mid}}

\displaystyle{=\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}\mid\sqrt{k!}\,\,a_{k}\frac{z^{k}}{\sqrt{k!}}\mid}

\leq(\displaystyle{\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}k!\mid a_{k}\mid^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}}

(\displaystyle{\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}\frac{\mid z\mid^{2k}}{k!})^{\frac{1}{2}}}

\leq\displaystyle{e^{\frac{\mid z\mid^{2}}{2}}\mid\mid\varphi\mid\mid_{\mathbb{B}}}.

\bullet
This inequality (i) says that pointwise evaluation is continuous, i.e. for each
z\in\mathbb{C}
, the map that takes a function
\varphi\in\mathbb{B}
to the number
\varphi(z)
is a continuous linear functional on
\mathbb{B}

(ii) Let
\varphi\in\mathbb{B}
then we have:

\varphi^{\prime}(z)=\displaystyle{\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}(k+1)a_{k+1}z^{k}=}\displaystyle{\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}\sqrt{k!}\sqrt{k+1}a_{k+1}\sqrt{k+1}\frac{z^{k}}{\sqrt{k!}}}

\displaystyle{=\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}\sqrt{(k+1)!}a_{k+1}\sqrt{k+1}\frac{z^{k}}{\sqrt{k!}}}

Then we get the following inequalities :

\mid\varphi^{\prime}(z)\mid^{2}\leq(\displaystyle{\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}\mid\sqrt{(k+1)!}a_{k+1}\sqrt{k+1}\frac{z^{k}}{\sqrt{k!}}\mid)^{2}}

By applying the Cauchy-Shwarz’s inequality, we obtain:

\leq(\displaystyle{\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}(k+1)!|a_{k+1}|^{2}\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}(k+1)\frac{|z|^{2k}}{k!}}

\leq(\displaystyle{||\varphi||_{\mathbb{B}}^{2}[\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}k\frac{|z|^{2k}}{k!}+e^{|z|^{2}}]}

\leq(\displaystyle{||\varphi||_{\mathbb{B}}^{2}[|z|^{2}e^{|z|^{2}}+e^{|z|^{2}}]}

i.e.

\displaystyle{\mid\varphi^{\prime}(z)\mid^{2}\leq(1+\mid z\mid^{2})e^{\mid z\mid^{2}}\mid\mid\varphi\mid\mid_{\mathbb{B}}^{2}.}

then

\displaystyle{\mid\varphi^{\prime}(z)\mid\leq\sqrt{1+\mid z\mid^{2}}e^{\frac{\mid z\mid^{2}}{2}}\mid\mid\varphi\mid\mid_{\mathbb{B}}}.

(iii) Let
\varphi\in\mathbb{B}
, then we have :

\varphi(z)=\displaystyle{\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}a_{k}z^{k}}

\varphi^{\prime}(z)=\displaystyle{\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}(k+1)a_{k+1}z^{k}}

\varphi^{\prime\prime}(z)=\displaystyle{\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}(k+1)(k+2)a_{k+2}z^{k}}

In similar, we obtain :

\mid\varphi^{\prime\prime}(z)\mid^{2}\leq(\displaystyle{\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}\mid\sqrt{k!}\sqrt{k+1}\sqrt{k+2}|a_{k+2}|\sqrt{k+1}\sqrt{k+2}\frac{|z|^{k}}{\sqrt{k!}}})^{2}

\displaystyle{\mid\varphi^{\prime\prime}(z)\mid^{2}\leq}

\displaystyle{\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}(k+2)!\mid a_{k+2}\mid^{2}}

\displaystyle{\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}(k^{2}+3k+2)\frac{\mid z\mid^{2k}}{k!}}

\displaystyle{\leq(\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1}\mid z\mid^{2}+\alpha_{2}\mid z\mid^{4})e^{\mid z\mid^{2}}\mid\mid\varphi\mid\mid_{\mathbb{B}}^{2}}
with
\alpha_{i}>0,i=0,1,2

Then:

\mid\varphi^{\prime\prime}(z)\mid\leq\sqrt{(\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1}\mid z\mid^{2}+\alpha_{2}\mid z\mid^{4})}e^{\frac{\mid z\mid^{2}}{2}}\mid\mid\varphi\mid\mid_{\mathbb{B}}

\bullet
(2)

For
z\in\mathbb{C}
, we can find a neighborhood
U
of
z
and a constant
C_{z}
such that

\displaystyle{|\varphi(\xi)|^{2}\leq C_{z}||\varphi||_{\mathbb{B}}^{2},\,\,\mbox{for all}\,\,\xi\in U\,\,\mbox{and all}\,\,\varphi\in\mathbb{B}}

(2.20)

Now, suppose that we have a sequence
\varphi_{n}\in\mathbb{B}
and
\varphi\in L_{2}(\mathbb{C},d\mu(z))
such that
\displaystyle{\varphi_{n}\longrightarrow\varphi\,\,in\,\,L_{2}(\mathbb{C},d\mu(z)),\,\,\mbox{as}\,\,n\longrightarrow+\infty}
.Then
\varphi_{n}
is certainly a Cauchy sequence in
L_{2}(\mathbb{C},d\mu(z))
; But then

\displaystyle{\underset{\xi\in U}{sup}|\varphi_{n}-\varphi_{m}|\sqrt{C_{z}}||\varphi_{n}-\varphi_{m}||_{L_{2}(\mathbb{C},d\mu(z))}\,\,\mbox{as}\,\,n,m\longrightarrow\infty}

(2.21)

This shows that the sequence
\varphi_{n}
converges locally uniformly to some limit function, which must be
\varphi
. A standard theorem shows that a locally uniform limit of holomorphic functions is always holomorphic. So the limit function
\varphi
is actually in
\mathbb{B}
. It follows that
\mathbb{B}
is closed.
\blacksquare

\rhd
Since
\mathbb{B}
is a closed subspace of
\displaystyle{L_{2}(\mathbb{C},d\mu(z))=\{\psi\,\,\text{measurable}\,\,;\int_{\mathbb{C}}|\psi(z)|^{2}d\mu(z)<\infty\}}
we have the following orthogonal decomposition:

\displaystyle{L_{2}(\mathbb{C},d\mu(z))=\mathbb{B}\oplus\mathbb{B}^{\perp}}

\rhd
The projection
\displaystyle{P:L_{2}(\mathbb{C},d\mu(z))\longrightarrow\mathbb{B}}
is the following operator :

\displaystyle{(P\psi)(z)=\frac{1}{\pi}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}<\psi,\frac{z^{n}}{\sqrt{n!}}>\frac{z^{n}}{\sqrt{n!}}\quad\quad(\psi\in L_{2}(\mathbb{C},d\mu(z)))}

or

\displaystyle{(P\psi)(z)=\frac{1}{2i\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}e^{z\overline{w}}\psi(w)e^{-|w|^{2}}dw\wedge d\overline{w}\quad(\psi\in L_{2}(\mathbb{C},d\mu(z)))}

where
\displaystyle{e^{z\overline{w}}}
is Bergman (reproducing) kernel with respect to Gaussian measure
\displaystyle{\frac{1}{2i\pi}e^{-|w|^{2}}dw\wedge d\overline{w}}

Definition 2.17
.

(Hilbert function spaces)

A Hilbert
function
space on a set
E
is a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
which is a subspace of the vector space of all functions
E\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
, with one additional and crucial property:

\bullet
For every
z\in E
the functional of
point evaluation
at
z
, given by
\varphi\mapsto\varphi(z)
, is a
bounded functional
on
\mathcal{H}

\rhd
Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert function space on a set
E
. As point evaluation is a bounded functional, then by the Riesz representation theorem there is, for every
z\in E
, an element
k_{z}\in\mathcal{H}
such that
\varphi(z)=\langle\varphi,k_{z}\rangle
for all
\varphi\in\mathcal{H}
.

Lemma 2.18
.

The Bargmann space
\mathbb{B}
is a Hilbert function space on
\mathbb{C}
.

Proof

To show that Bargmann space
\mathbb{B}
is a Hilbert function space on
\mathbb{C}
, we need to show that for every
z\in\mathbb{C}
, the functional
\varphi\mapsto\varphi(z)
is bounded. From (i) of above proposition, this functional is bounded, then
\mathbb{B}
is a Hilbert function space on
\mathbb{C}
and by the Riesz representation theorem there exists some
k_{z}\in\mathbb{B}
such that
\displaystyle{\varphi(z)=\langle\varphi,k_{z}\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{C}}\varphi(w)\overline{k_{z}(w)}d\mu(w)}
for each
\varphi\in\mathbb{B}
.
\blacksquare

Definition 2.19
.

(Reproducing kernels)

The function
k_{z}
is called the kernel function at
z
.

We define a function
\displaystyle{K:E\times E\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}}
by
K(z,w)=k_{w}(z)
, the function
K
is called the reproducing kernel of
\mathcal{H}
.

The family of functions
k_{w}=K(\cdot,w)
enable one to reproduce the values of any
\varphi\in\mathbb{H}
via the relationship
\varphi(w)=\langle\varphi,k_{w}\rangle
. Note that
K(z,w)=\langle k_{w},k_{z}\rangle

The reproducing kernel
K(z,w)
is positive definite if for all choices of
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
z_{1},...,z_{N}\in E
and
c_{1},...,c_{N}\in\mathbb{C}
, we have:

\displaystyle{||\sum_{j=1}^{N}k(.,z)c_{j}||_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{N}\overline{c_{i}}k(z_{j},z_{j})c_{j}\geq 0}

Definition 2.20
.

(Reproducing kernel Hilbert space[RKHS])

Given a set
E
, we will say that
\mathcal{H}
is a reproducing kernel Hilbert space(RKHS) on
E
over
\mathbb{K}
where
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{C}
or
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{R}
, provided that:

(i)
\mathcal{H}
is a vector vector space,

(ii)
\mathcal{H}
is endowed with an inner product,
\langle,\rangle
, making it into a Hilbert space,

(iii) for every
z\in E
, the linear evaluation functional,
\displaystyle{ev_{z}:\mathcal{H}\longrightarrow,\mathbb{K}}
defined by
\displaystyle{\varphi\longrightarrow ev(\varphi)=\varphi(z)}
, is bounded.

Remark 2.21
.

\displaystyle{||ev_{z}||^{2}=||k_{z}||^{2}=\langle k_{z},k_{z}\rangle=K(z,z)}
.

2.4
Examples of reproducing kernel spaces

\rhd_{1}

\mathbb{C}^{n}
can be considered as a Hilbert function space on
\displaystyle{E=\{1;2,..,n\}}
. The vector
\displaystyle{z=(z_{1},z_{2},.....,z_{n})}
corresponds to the function
\displaystyle{\varphi:E\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}}
defined by
\displaystyle{\varphi(j)=z_{j}}
.

As
\mathbb{C}^{n}
is finite dimensional then all linear functional is continuous, and therefore
\mathbb{C}^{n}
is a Hilbert function space. We have for each
z\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
, and for
1jn

z_{j}=<z,e_{j}>
where
e_{j}
is the vector containing a
1
in position
j
and
0
elsewhere.

Then
k_{j}=e_{j}
and

K(i,j)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}1\quad\mbox{if}\,\,i=j\\
\\
0\quad\mbox{if}\,\,i\neq j\\
\end{array}\right.

(2.22)

\blacksquare

\rhd_{2}
Let
\displaystyle{\ell^{2}(E)=\{\varphi:E\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}\,\,\mbox{such that}\,\,\sum_{z\in E}|\varphi(z)|^{2}<\infty\}}

For each
z\in E
and for each
\displaystyle{\varphi\in\ell^{2}(E)}
, we have

|\varphi(z)|=\sqrt{|\varphi(z)|^{2}}\leq\sqrt{\sum_{w\in E}|\varphi(w)|^{2}}=||\varphi||_{\ell^{2}(E)}

(2.23)

and therefore the evaluation functional at
z
is bounded. That
\displaystyle{\ell^{2}(E)}
is a Hilbert function space and for each
\varphi\in\ell^{2}(E)
and for each
z\in E
, we have

\displaystyle{\varphi(z)=<\varphi,e_{z}>}
where
\displaystyle{e_{z}:E\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}}
is the function defined by

e_{z}(w)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}1\quad\mbox{if}\,\,w=z\\
\\
0\quad\mbox{if}\,\,w\neq z\\
\end{array}\right.

(2.24)

It follows that
k_{z}=e_{z}
and

K(z,w)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}1\quad\mbox{if}\,\,z=w\\
\\
0\quad\mbox{if}\,\,z\neq w\\
\end{array}\right.

(2.25)

\blacksquare

\rhd_{3}
The Hardy space
\mathcal{H}^{2}
is the class of holomorphic functions
\varphi
on the open unit disk
\mathbb{D}
satisfy:

\noindent\displaystyle{\varphi(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}z^{n}\,\,,||\varphi||_{\mathcal{H}^{2}}^{2}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}|a_{n}|^{2}<\infty}

(2.26)

Let
w\in\mathbb{D}
. If
\displaystyle{\varphi(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}z^{n}}
then by using Cauchy-Schwarz inequality we get

\noindent\displaystyle{|\varphi(w)|\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}|a_{n}w^{n}|\leq\sqrt{\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}|a_{n}|^{2}}\sqrt{\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}|w|^{2n}}=\sqrt{\frac{1}{1-|w|^{2}}}\,\,||\varphi||_{\mathcal{H}^{2}}}

(2.27)

Hence, the evaluation functional at
w
is bounded. That
\mathcal{H}^{2}
is a Hilbert function space and for each
\varphi\in\mathcal{H}^{2}
and for each
z\in\mathbb{D}
, we have

\begin{array}[]{c}\displaystyle{<\varphi,k_{w}>=\varphi(w)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}w^{n}=<\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}z^{n},\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\overline{w}^{n}z^{n}>}\\
\displaystyle{=<\varphi,\frac{1}{1-z\overline{w}}>}\end{array}

(2.28)

We have used the Taylor series :

\displaystyle{\frac{1}{1-u}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}u^{n}}

(2.29)

Then

\displaystyle{k_{w}(z)=\frac{1}{1-z\overline{w}}}
and
\displaystyle{K(z,w)=k_{w}(z)=\frac{1}{1-z\overline{w}}}
.
\blacksquare

\rhd_{4}
The Bergman space
\mathcal{A}^{2}
is the class of holomorphic functions
\varphi
on the open unit disk
\mathbb{D}
satisfy:

\noindent\displaystyle{\varphi(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}z^{n}\,\,,||\varphi||_{\mathcal{A}^{2}}^{2}=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{D}}|\varphi(z)|^{2}dxdy<\infty}

(2.30)

By Parseval’s formula and the monotone convergence theorem, we deduce that

\displaystyle{\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{D}}|\varphi(z)|^{2}dxdy=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}|\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}r^{n}e^{in\theta}|^{2}rdrd\theta=2\int_{0}^{1}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}|a_{n}|^{2}r^{2n+1}dr}

\displaystyle{=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{|a_{n}|^{2}}{n+1}}

Hence

\displaystyle{\varphi\in\mathcal{A}^{2}\iff\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{|a_{n}|^{2}}{n+1}<\infty}

and

\displaystyle{<\varphi,\psi>_{\mathcal{A}^{2}}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{a_{n}\overline{b_{n}}}{n+1}}

where
\displaystyle{\varphi(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}z^{n}}
and
\displaystyle{\psi(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}b_{n}z^{n}}

\displaystyle{(\mathcal{A}^{2},\,\,<.>_{\mathcal{A}^{2}})}
is a Hilbert space

Let
w\in\mathbb{D}
. If
\displaystyle{\varphi(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}z^{n}}
then by using Cauchy-Schwaerz inequality we get

\noindent\displaystyle{|\varphi(w)|\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}|a_{n}w^{n}|\leq\sqrt{\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{|a_{n}|^{2}}{n+1}}\sqrt{\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(n+1)|w|^{2n}}=\sqrt{\frac{1}{(1-|w|^{2})^{2}}}\,\,||\varphi||_{\mathcal{A}^{2}}}

(2.31)

We have used the Taylor series :

\displaystyle{\frac{1}{(1-u)^{2}}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}nu^{n-1}}

(2.32)

Hence, the evaluation functional at
w
is bounded. That
\mathcal{A}^{2}
is a Hilbert function space and for each
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}^{2}
and for each
z\in\mathbb{D}
, we have

\displaystyle{<\varphi,k_{w}>_{\mathcal{A}^{2}}=\varphi(w)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}w^{n}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{a_{n}\overline{(n+1)\overline{w}^{n}}}{n+1}=<\varphi,\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(n+1)(z\overline{w})^{n}>}

Then

\displaystyle{k_{w}(z)=\frac{1}{(1-z\overline{w})^{2}}}
and
\displaystyle{K(z,w)=k_{w}(z)=\frac{1}{(1-z\overline{w})^{2}}}
.
\blacksquare

Lemma 2.22
.

The Bargmann space
\mathbb{B}
is a reproducing kernel Hilbert space with reproducing kernel
\displaystyle{K(z,w)=e^{z\overline{w}}}
; i.e., for every
\varphi\in\mathbb{B}
we have

\displaystyle{\varphi(z)=<\varphi,K(z,.)>_{\mathbb{B}}}

(2.33)

Proof

The Bergmann space
\mathbb{B}
is the class of entire functions
\varphi
on
\mathbb{C}
satisfy:

\noindent\displaystyle{\varphi(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}z^{n}\,\,,||\varphi||_{\mathbb{B}}^{2}=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}|\varphi(z)|^{2}e^{-|z|^{2}}dxdy<\infty}

(2.34)

By Fubini theorem and the monotone convergence theorem, we deduce that

\displaystyle{\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}|\varphi(z)|^{2}e^{-|z|^{2}}dxdy=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{2\pi}|\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}r^{n}e^{in\theta}|^{2}rdrd\theta}
=
\displaystyle{=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}n!|a_{n}|^{2}}

Hence

\displaystyle{\varphi\in\mathbb{B}\iff\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}n!|a_{n}|^{2}<\infty}

and

\displaystyle{<\varphi,\psi>_{\mathbb{B}}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}n!a_{n}\overline{b_{n}}}

where
\displaystyle{\varphi(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}z^{n}}
and
\displaystyle{\psi(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}b_{n}z^{n}}

\displaystyle{(\mathbb{B},\,\,<.>_{\mathbb{B}})}
is a Hilbert space

Let
w\in\mathbb{C}
. If
\displaystyle{\varphi(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}z^{n}}
then by using Cauchy-Schwarz inequality we get

\noindent\displaystyle{|\varphi(w)|\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}|a_{n}w^{n}|\leq\sqrt{\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{|w|^{2n}}{n!}}\sqrt{\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}n!|a_{n}|^{2}}=\sqrt{e^{|w|^{2}}}\,\,||\varphi||_{\mathbb{B}}}

(2.35)

Hence, the evaluation functional at
w
is bounded, which implies

\displaystyle{|\varphi(w)-\psi(w)|\leq\sqrt{e^{|w|^{2}}}\,\,||\varphi-\psi||_{\mathbb{B}}\,\,\mbox{for all }\,\,\varphi,\psi\in\mathbb{B}}

(2.36)

and

For all
w\in\mathbb{C}
and for all
\psi\in\mathbb{B}
, it holds that:

\noindent\displaystyle{\forall\,\,\varphi\in\mathbb{B},\,\,\forall\,\,\epsilon>0\,\,\exists\,\,\delta>0\,\,\mbox{;}\,\,(||\varphi-\psi||_{\mathbb{B}}<\delta\Longrightarrow|\varphi(w)-\psi(w)|<\epsilon)}

(2.37)

i.e. all the point evaluation maps are continuous.

That
\mathbb{B}
is a Hilbert function space and for each
\varphi\in\mathbb{B}
and for each
z\in\mathbb{C}
, we have

\displaystyle{<\varphi,k_{w}>_{\mathbb{B}}=\varphi(w)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}w^{n}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}n!a_{n}\overline{\frac{\overline{w}^{n}}{n!}}=<\varphi,\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(z\overline{w})^{n}}{n!}>}

Then

\displaystyle{k_{w}(z)=e^{z\overline{w}}}
and
\displaystyle{K(z,w)=k_{w}(z)=e^{z\overline{w}}}
.
\blacksquare

Proposition 2.23
.

Let
\displaystyle{(z,w)\in\mathbb{C}^{2},u\in\mathbb{R}\,\,\mbox{ and}\,\,\mathcal{A}(z,u)=e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2}+\sqrt{2}uz-\frac{z^{2}}{2}}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{z^{n}}{\sqrt{n!}}h_{n}(u)}

where
(h_{n}(u))_{n\geq 0}
denote the normalized Hermite functions.

Then

\displaystyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{A}(z,u)\overline{\mathcal{A}(w,u)}du=\sqrt{\pi}e^{z\overline{w}}}

(2.38)

Proof

\displaystyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{A}(z,u)\overline{\mathcal{A}(w,u)}du=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2}+\sqrt{2}uz-\frac{z^{2}}{2}}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2}+\sqrt{2}u\overline{w}-\frac{\overline{w}^{2}}{2}}du}

\displaystyle{=e^{-\frac{1}{2}(z^{2}+\overline{w}^{2}}e^{\frac{(z+\overline{w})^{2}}{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-(u-\frac{z+\overline{w}}{\sqrt{2}})^{2}}du}

\displaystyle{=e^{z\overline{w}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-t^{2}}dt}

\displaystyle{=\sqrt{\pi}e^{z\overline{w}}}

where
\displaystyle{t=u-\frac{z+\overline{w}}{\sqrt{2}}}
.
\blacksquare

It is advisable to consult at this topic
[3]
.

Remark 2.24
.

1. Polynomials and entire functions of exponential type belong to Bargmann space. For example, sinc function
\displaystyle{\frac{sinz}{z}}
is entire function of exponential type (see
[42]
or
[46]
). Hence it belongs to Bargmann space.

2.
\displaystyle{\sigma(z)=z\prod(1-\frac{z}{\lambda_{m,n}})\exp(\frac{z}{\lambda_{m,n}}+\frac{z^{2}}{2\lambda_{m,n}^{2}})}
, is Weierstrass
\sigma
- function and
\lambda_{m,n}
are lattice points in
\mathbb{C}
.

Under suitable conditions on lattice points,
\displaystyle{\frac{\sigma(z)}{z}}
belongs to Bargmann space see
[8]
.

2.5
Fundamental results on reproducing kernel spaces

Proposition 2.25
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a reproducing kernel Hilbert space on a set
E
and
K(z,w)
is its reproducing kernel. Let
\mathcal{H}_{0}
be denote the space of finite linear combinations

\displaystyle{\sum_{j}\alpha_{j}K_{z_{j}},\alpha_{j}\in\mathbb{C}\,\,\mbox{and}\,\,z_{j}\in E}\,\\
,i.e.\,\,\mathcal{H}_{0}=span\{k_{z},z\in E\}

(2.39)

then

\mathcal{H}_{0}
is dense in
\mathcal{H}
and it follows that
\mathcal{H}
is determined by the reproducing kernel.

Proof

Let
\displaystyle{\varphi\in span\{k_{z},z\in E\}^{\perp}}
. Then for all
z\in E
, we have
\varphi(z)=<\varphi,k_{z}>=0
this implies that
\varphi=0
and
\displaystyle{span\{k_{z},z\in E\}^{\perp}=\{0\}}
i.e.
\mathcal{H}_{0}
is dense in
\mathcal{H}
.
\blacksquare

Theorem 2.26
.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{1}
and
\mathcal{H}_{2}
be two reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces on
E
with a
same
kernel then they are
equal
.

Proof

As
\mathcal{H}_{1}
and
\mathcal{H}_{2}
are two reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces on
E
with
same
kernel then we have
\displaystyle{||\varphi||_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}=||\varphi||_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}}
for all
\displaystyle{\varphi\in span\{k_{z},z\in E\}}
. In fact :

If
\displaystyle{\varphi=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}k_{z_{j}}}
then we have

\displaystyle{||\varphi||_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}^{2}=<\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}k_{z_{j}},\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}k_{z_{i}}>=\sum_{i,j}^{n}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha_{i}}<k_{z_{j}},k_{z_{j}}>_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}=\sum_{i,j}^{n}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha_{i}}K(z_{i},z_{j})}
.

Similar, we get

\displaystyle{||\varphi||_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}^{2}=\sum_{i,j}^{n}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha_{i}}K(z_{i},z_{j})}
.

Now let
\psi\in\mathcal{H}_{1}
then from above lemma, we deduce there exists an sequence
\displaystyle{\psi_{n}\in span\{k_{z},z\in E\}}
such that
\displaystyle{\psi_{n}\longrightarrow\varphi\,\,\mbox{in}\mathcal{H}_{1}}
. As
(\psi_{n})
is convergent then in particular, it is a Cauchy sequence :

\displaystyle{\forall\,\,\epsilon>0,\,\exists\,\,N>0;\,\ \forall\,\ n>N,\,\ \forall\,\ m>N\Longrightarrow||\psi_{n}-\psi_{m}||_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}<\epsilon}

But
\displaystyle{||\psi_{n}-\psi_{m}||_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}=||\psi_{n}-\psi_{m}||_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}}
thus
(\psi_{n})
is Cauchy sequence in
\mathcal{H}_{2}
, so converges to some
\varphi\in\mathcal{H}_{2}
because
\mathcal{H}_{2}
is complete.

But the convergence in an reproducing kernel Hilbert space (RKHS) implies the “simple convergence.” So
\psi_{n}\longrightarrow\psi
as
n\longrightarrow+\infty
and
\psi_{n}\longrightarrow\varphi
as
n\longrightarrow+\infty
. So
\psi=\varphi
and
\psi\in\mathcal{H}_{2}
. By the same reasoning, any function in
\mathcal{H}_{2}
is in
\mathcal{H}_{1}
.

Finally, we have

\displaystyle{||\psi||_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}=\lim\limits_{n\longrightarrow+\infty}||\psi_{n}||_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}=\lim\limits_{n\longrightarrow+\infty}||\psi_{n}||_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}=||\psi||_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}}
.
\blacksquare

Theorem 2.27
.

(
Moore
)

Let
E
be a set and
K:E\times E\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
a semi-definite function positive. Then there is a unique RKHS on
E
whose kernel is
K
.

Proof

Uniqueness follows immediately from above theorem. Let us pose, for
z,w\in E,k_{w}(z)=K(z,w)
and
\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}_{0}=span\{k_{z},z\in E\}}

If
\displaystyle{\varphi(z)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}k_{z_{i}},}
and
\displaystyle{\psi(z)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\beta_{j}k_{w_{j}},}
then we define

\displaystyle{<\varphi,\psi>=\ \sum_{i,j}\alpha_{i}\overline{\beta_{j}}K(w_{j},z_{i})}

(2.40)

We must first verify that
<,>
is well defined. suppose that

\displaystyle{\varphi(z)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}k_{z_{i}},}

\displaystyle{=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}^{{}^{\prime}}k_{z_{i}^{{}^{\prime}}},}

\displaystyle{\psi(z)=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\beta_{j}k_{w_{j}},}

\displaystyle{=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\beta_{j}^{{}^{\prime}}k_{w_{j}^{{}^{\prime}}},}

are two different representations for
\varphi
and
\psi
(we can assume that there are the same number of terms in representations by adding null coefficients if necessary). So for each
z\in E
, we have

\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}k_{z_{i}},}

\displaystyle{=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}^{{}^{\prime}}k_{z_{i}^{{}^{\prime}}},}

\displaystyle{\sum_{j=1}^{m}\beta_{j}k_{w_{j}},}

\displaystyle{=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\beta_{j}^{{}^{\prime}}k_{w_{j}^{{}^{\prime}}},}

and so

\displaystyle{\sum_{i,j}\alpha_{i}\overline{\beta_{j}}K(w_{j},z_{i})=\sum_{j=1}^{m}.\overline{\beta_{j}}(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}k_{z_{i}}(w_{j})=\sum_{j=1}^{m}.\overline{\beta_{j}}(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}^{{}^{\prime}}k_{z_{i}^{{}^{\prime}}}(w_{j})}

\displaystyle{=\sum_{i,j}\alpha_{i}^{{}^{\prime}}\overline{\beta_{j}}\overline{K(z_{i}^{{}^{\prime}},w_{j})}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}^{{}^{\prime}}(\overline{\sum_{j=1}^{m}\beta_{j}k_{w_{j}}(z_{i}^{{}^{\prime}}}})

\displaystyle{=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}^{{}^{\prime}}(\overline{\sum_{j=1}^{m}\beta_{j}^{{}^{\prime}}k_{w_{j}}(z_{i}^{{}^{\prime}}})=\sum_{i,j}\alpha_{i}^{{}^{\prime}}\beta_{j}^{{}^{\prime}}K(w_{j},z_{ji}^{{}^{\prime}})}

Thus,
<,>
is well defined. We verify that
<,>
is left linear and “anti-symmetric”. Moreover, as
K\geq 0
, we have
<\varphi,\varphi>\geq 0
for all
\varphi\in\mathcal{H}_{0}
. Note that for all
\varphi\in\mathcal{H}_{0}
, we have
<\varphi,k_{z}>=\varphi(z)
.

Now suppose that
<\varphi,\varphi>=0
. By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality (which is valid for positive semidefinite anti-symmetric bilinear forms),

|<\varphi,k_{z}>|\leq<\varphi,\varphi><k_{z},k_{z}>=0

So, for all
z\in E

\varphi(z)=<\varphi,k_{z}>=0

So
\varphi=0
. So
<,>
is a inner product.
\blacksquare

We define
\mathcal{H}
as being the completion of
\mathcal{H}_{0}
with respect to the scalar product. We can see
\mathcal{H}
as a space of functions on
E
. Indeed, if
\varphi\in\mathcal{H}
, then we associate to it the function
\tilde{\varphi}
defined by

\tilde{\varphi}(z):=<\varphi,k_{z}>

(2.41)

This uniquely defines
\tilde{\varphi}
because
\mathcal{H}_{0}
is dense in
\mathcal{H}
. The set
\displaystyle{\{\tilde{\varphi};\varphi\in\mathcal{H}\}}
is a subspace of the vector space
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{F}(E,\mathbb{C})}
of functions on
E
with values in
\mathbb{C}
. This is isomorphic (as a vector space) to
\mathcal{H}
. Hence
\mathcal{H}
is an reproducing kernel Hilbert space (RKHS) with kernel
K
.

3
Unitarity of the Segal-Bargmann transform and some properties of its adjoint

3.1
On gaussian measure and Bargmann space

Let be
O(\mathbb{C})
the set of entire functions,
\mathfrak{M}
is the operator of multiplication by
z=x+iy;(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
i^{2}=-1
(i.e
\mathfrak{M}\varphi=z\varphi,\varphi\in O(\mathbb{C})
) and
\mathfrak{D}
is the operator of derivative in order
z
(i.e
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{D}\varphi=\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\varphi,\varphi\in O(\mathbb{C})}
). we observe that on
O(\mathbb{C})
the operators
\mathfrak{D}
and
\mathfrak{M}
satisfy :

\displaystyle{[\mathfrak{D},\mathfrak{M}]=I}

(3.1)

Lemma 3.1
.

(
Gaussian measure
)

If we request that
\mathfrak{M}
is adjoint to
\mathfrak{D}
in
O(\mathbb{C})
with inner product :

\displaystyle{<\varphi,\psi>=\int_{\mathbb{C}}\varphi(z)\overline{\psi(z)}\rho(z,\bar{z})dxdy}
where
\rho(z,\bar{z})
is a measure.

Then
\displaystyle{\rho(z,\bar{z})=e^{-\mid z\mid^{2}}}
(Gaussian measure).

Proof

The requirement that
\mathfrak{M}=\mathfrak{D}^{*}
gives :

\displaystyle{\int_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}[\varphi(z)]\overline{\psi(z)}\rho((z,\bar{z})dxdy=\int_{\mathbb{C}}\varphi(z)\overline{z\psi(z)}\rho((z,\bar{z})dxdy}

As

\displaystyle{\frac{\partial}{\partial z}[\varphi(z)]\overline{\psi(z)}\rho((z,\bar{z})=\frac{\partial}{\partial z}[\varphi(z)\overline{\psi(z)}\rho((z,\bar{z})]-\varphi(z)\frac{\partial}{\partial z}[\overline{\psi(z)}]\rho((z,\bar{z})-\varphi(z)\overline{\psi(z)}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}[\rho((z,\bar{z})]}

then

\displaystyle{\int_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}[\varphi(z)]\overline{\psi(z)}\rho((z,\bar{z})dxdy=\int_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}[\varphi(z)\overline{\psi(z)}\rho((z,\bar{z})]dxdy-\int_{\mathbb{C}}\varphi(z)\frac{\partial}{\partial z}[\overline{\psi(z)}]\rho((z,\bar{z})dxdy}

\displaystyle{-\int_{\mathbb{C}}\varphi(z)\overline{\psi(z)}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}[\rho((z,\bar{z})]dxdy}

In the right hand side, the first term of the integrand vanishes if we assume that the inner product between
\varphi
and
\psi
is finite, so that
\varphi\overline{\psi}\rho\longrightarrow 0
sufficiently fast as
\mid z\mid\longrightarrow\infty
. The second term also vanishes,because
\psi
is holomorphic, so that
\overline{\psi}
is anti-holomorphic and hence
\displaystyle{\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\overline{\psi}=0}
.This gives:

\displaystyle{\int_{\mathbb{C}}\varphi(z)\overline{z\psi(z)}\rho((z,\bar{z})dxdy+\int_{\mathbb{C}}\varphi(z)\frac{\partial}{\partial z}[\overline{\psi(z)}]\rho((z,\bar{z})dxdy=0}\\

(3.2)

which is solved for arbitrary
\varphi
and
\psi
if

\displaystyle{\bar{z}\rho((z,\bar{z})+\frac{\partial}{\partial z}[\rho((z,\bar{z})]=0}\\

(3.3)

giving

\displaystyle{\rho((z,\bar{z})=Ce^{-\mid z\mid^{2}}}\\

(3.4)

The constant
C
is chosen to be
\displaystyle{\frac{1}{\pi}}
, so that the norm of the constant function
\varphi(z)\equiv 1
is one. This explains why the space of holomorphic functions equipped with a Gaussian measure gives Bargmann space
\mathbb{B}
.
\blacksquare

\bullet
It is well known that the classical Bargmann space is the only space of entire functions on which the creation and annihilation operators are adjoints of
each others and satisfy the classical commutation rules. Of course, this is not true anymore on
L_{2}(\mathbb{C},d\mu)
, see Proposition 7.2 in
[37]
.

3.2
Some properties of Segal-Bargmann transform

Definition 3.2
.

(
Segal-Bargmann transform
)

The Segal-Bargmann transform
\displaystyle{SB:L_{2}(\mathbb{R})\longrightarrow\mathbb{B}:f\longrightarrow\varphi}
defined by

\displaystyle{SB[f](z)=\varphi(z)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{A}(z,u)f(u)du,\quad\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Large(\star)}}}

(3.5)

with

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}(z,u)={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}c}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2}+\sqrt{2}uz-\frac{z^{2}}{2}};\,\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}c>0}\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Large(\star\star)}}}

(3.6)

Remark 3.3
.

(i) The above transformation was introduced by Bargmann in (
[4], p. 198
) with
\displaystyle{c=\frac{1}{\pi^{\frac{1}{4}}}}

(ii) Let
u=\sqrt{2}q
then
\displaystyle{du=\sqrt{2}dq}
and

\displaystyle{SB[f](z)=c^{\prime}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-q^{2}+2qz-\frac{z^{2}}{2}}f(q)dq;\,\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}c^{\prime}=c\sqrt{2}}}

(3.7)

(iii) Different forms of this transformation were used in the literature in particular, a parametrized form of the Bargmann transform given by K. Zhu
[48]
as follows:

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}:L_{2}(\mathbb{R})\longrightarrow\mathbb{B}_{\alpha}\subset L_{2}(\mathbb{C},d\mu_{\alpha}(z):f\longrightarrow\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}[f](z)}}

where
\mathbb{B}_{\alpha}
is the subspace of all entire functions in
L_{2}(\mathbb{C},d\mu_{\alpha}(z))
with respect to the weight

\displaystyle{\mu_{\alpha}(z)=\frac{\alpha}{\pi}e^{-\alpha|z|^{2}};\alpha>0}

(3.8)

as follows

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}[f](z)=(\frac{2\alpha}{\pi})^{\frac{1}{4}}e^{\frac{\alpha}{2}z^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-\alpha(z-u)^{2}}f(u)du,\,\,z\in\mathbb{C}}

(3.9)

provided that the integral is finite.

(iv)
\bullet_{1}
Monograph on Fock spaces
[48]
covers many properties of the Bargmann transform in its general normalization with
\alpha>0
.

\bullet_{2}
Analysis of the Bose-Einstein condensation in the semi-classical limit in
[1]
uses
\displaystyle{\alpha=\frac{1}{\sqrt{h}}}
with the small parameter
h>0
.

\bullet_{3}
Introduction of the Bargmann transform in harmonic analysis in (
[13]
, Section 1.6] uses
\alpha=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}

\bullet_{4}
The work on the lowest Landau level in
[15]
uses
\alpha=1

\bullet_{5}
It is advisable to consult at this topic the recent paper
[36]
.

\bullet_{6}
Let
\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}(z,u)=e^{\frac{\alpha}{2}z^{2}-\alpha(z-u)^{2}=e^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}z^{2}+2\alpha uz-\alpha u^{2}}}=e^{-\frac{(\sqrt{\alpha}z)^{2}}{2}+2\sqrt{\alpha}u\sqrt{\alpha}z-\alpha u^{2}}}
,

\displaystyle{[\mathbb{I}_{\alpha}f](z)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{N}(z,u)f(u)du}
and
\xi=\sqrt{\alpha}z
. Then

\displaystyle{\tilde{\mathcal{N}}(\xi,u)=\mathcal{N}(\sqrt{\alpha}z,u)=e^{-\frac{\xi^{2}}{2}+2\sqrt{\alpha}u\xi-\alpha u^{2}}}
,

\displaystyle{e^{-\alpha|z|^{2}}dz\Lambda d\overline{z}=\frac{1}{\alpha}e^{-|\xi|^{2}}d\xi\Lambda d\overline{\xi}}

and

\displaystyle{[\mathbb{I}_{\alpha}f](\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\tilde{\mathcal{N}}(\xi,u)f(u)du}
.

If we use
\alpha=\frac{1}{2}
, we obtain the kernel of classical Bargmann transform :

\displaystyle{\tilde{\mathcal{N}}(\xi,u)=e^{-\frac{\xi^{2}}{2}+\sqrt{2}u\xi-\frac{u^{2}}{2}}}
,
\xi\in\mathbb{C}
and
u\in\mathbb{R}
.

\rhd
It is usefull to observe that the following linear transform

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}:L_{2}(\mathbb{R})\longrightarrow L_{2}(\mathbb{R},e^{-u^{2}}du)}
,
\displaystyle{f\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}(f)=e^{\frac{u^{2}}{2}}f}

is a unitary isomorphism.

\rhd
We recall that in
L_{2}(\mathbb{R})
representation, the operators
\displaystyle{a=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\frac{\partial}{\partial u}+u)}
and
\displaystyle{a^{*}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(-\frac{\partial}{\partial u}+u)}
are adjoint with respect to the
L_{2}(\mathbb{R})
-inner product and satisfy
[a,a^{*}]=I
.

The Segal-Bargmann transform was introduced (depently) by I. Segal and V. Bargmann near 1960 and also by F.A Berezin in the same time give a generalization of this transformation). It has to satisfy the following two diagrams:

\displaystyle{\begin{array}[]{c}L_{2}(\mathbb{R})\quad\longrightarrow^{\underset{\longrightarrow}{SB}}\longrightarrow\quad\mathbb{B}\\
\quad\quad\downarrow a\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\downarrow\mathfrak{D}\\
L_{2}(\mathbb{R})\quad\longrightarrow^{\underset{\longrightarrow}{SB}}\longrightarrow\quad\mathbb{B}\\
\end{array}}
and
\displaystyle{\begin{array}[]{c}L_{2}(\mathbb{R})\quad\longrightarrow^{\underset{\longrightarrow}{SB}}\longrightarrow\quad\mathbb{B}\\
\quad\quad\downarrow a^{*}\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\downarrow\mathfrak{M}\\
L_{2}(\mathbb{R})\quad\longrightarrow^{\underset{\longrightarrow}{SB}}\longrightarrow\quad\mathbb{B}\\
\end{array}}

Lemma 3.4
.

(i)\,\,\displaystyle{\mathfrak{D}A(z,u)=a^{*}A(z,u)}

(ii)\,\,\displaystyle{\mathfrak{M}A(z,u)=aA(z,u)}

(iii)\,\,\displaystyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}}A(z,u)\overline{A(\xi,u)}du=ce^{z\overline{\xi}},c>0}

Proof

(i) As
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{D}A(z,u)=\frac{\partial}{\partial z}A(z,u)=(-z+\sqrt{2}u)A(z,u)}

and

\displaystyle{a^{*}A(z,u)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(-\frac{\partial}{\partial u}+u)A(z,u)=(-z+\sqrt{2}u)A(z,u)}

Then
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{D}A(z,u)=a^{*}A(z,u)}

(ii) As
\displaystyle{\frac{\partial}{\partial u}A(z,u)=(-u+\sqrt{2}z)A(z,u)}
then
\displaystyle{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\frac{\partial}{\partial u}+u)A(z,u)=zA(z,u)}
.

Hence
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{M}A(z,u)=aA(z,u)}

(iii)
\displaystyle{<\mathcal{A}(z,u),\mathcal{A}(\xi,u)>_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R})}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}A(z,u)\overline{A(\xi,u)}du}

\displaystyle{=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2}+\sqrt{2}uz-\frac{z^{2}}{2}}\overline{e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2}+\sqrt{2}u\xi-\frac{\xi^{2}}{2}}}du}

\displaystyle{=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2}+\sqrt{2}uz-\frac{z^{2}}{2}}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2}+\sqrt{2}u\overline{\xi}-\frac{\overline{\xi}^{2}}{2}}du}
v

\displaystyle{=e^{-\frac{z^{2}}{2}}e^{-\frac{\overline{\xi}^{2}}{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2}+\sqrt{2}uz}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2}+\sqrt{2}u\overline{\xi}}du}

\displaystyle{=e^{-\frac{1}{2}(z^{2}+\overline{\xi}^{2})}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-u^{2}+\sqrt{2}u(z+\overline{\xi})}du}

\displaystyle{=e^{-\frac{1}{2}(z^{2}+\overline{\xi}^{2})}e^{\frac{(z+\overline{\xi})^{2}}{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-(u-\frac{z+\overline{\xi}}{\sqrt{2}})^{2}}du}

\displaystyle{=e^{z\overline{\xi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-t^{2}}dt}

\displaystyle{=\sqrt{\pi}e^{z\overline{\xi}}}

where
\displaystyle{t=u-\frac{z+\overline{\xi}}{\sqrt{2}}}
.
\blacksquare

Corollary 3.5
.

(i)
\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}(z,u)}
is in
L_{2}(\mathbb{R})
for all
z\in\mathbb{C}

(ii)
\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}(z,u)f(u)}
is integrable for
f\in L_{2}(\mathbb{R})

(iii)
\displaystyle{|SB(f)(z)|\leq\sqrt{\pi}e^{z\overline{z}}}

Proof

(i) From (iii) of above lemma we get that, for every
z
in
\mathbb{C}
, we have

\displaystyle{<A(z,u),A(z,u)>_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R})}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}A(z,u)\overline{A(z,u)}du=\sqrt{\pi}e^{z\overline{z}}}

Therefore
\displaystyle{A(z,u)}
is in
L_{2}(\mathbb{R})
for all
z\in\mathbb{C}

(ii) Combining (i) with Cauchy-Schwarz’s inequality and the fact that
f
in
L_{2}(\mathbb{R})
implies
\overline{f}
in
L_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, we find that
\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}(z,u)f(u)}
is integrable for
f\in L_{2}(\mathbb{R})
i.e.
\displaystyle{SB(f)(z)}
is well defined for all
f
in
L_{2}(\mathbb{R})

(iii) From Schwarz’ inequality it also follows that
\displaystyle{|SB(f)(z)|\leq\sqrt{\pi}e^{z\overline{z}}}
since
\displaystyle{||A(z,u)||_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R})}=\sqrt{\pi}e^{z\overline{z}}}
.
\blacksquare

Theorem 6.2.3

The Segal-Bargmann transform
\displaystyle{SB:L_{2}(\mathbb{R})\longrightarrow\mathbb{B}:f\longrightarrow\varphi}
defined by

\displaystyle{SB[f](z)=\varphi(z)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{A}(z,u)f(u)du}
with
\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}(z,u)=e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2}+\sqrt{2}uz-\frac{z^{2}}{2}}}
is a holomorphic function and it is injective.

Proof

\rhd
Showing that for any
f\in L_{2}(\mathbb{R})
the image
SBf
is a holomorphic function. We will use the following classical theorem:

Theorem 3.6
.

(
Classical theorem
)

Let
h:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
such that:

(1) For all
z\in\mathbb{C}
the function
u\longrightarrow h(u,z)
is
integrable
.

(2) For all
u\in\mathbb{R}
the function
z\longrightarrow h(u,z)
is
holomorphic
.

(3) For each
z_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
there exists an open neighbourhood
U
of
z_{0}
and a
non-negative
function
g\in L_{1}(\mathbb{R})
(the space of integrable functions) such that for all
z\in U

\displaystyle{|h(u,z)|\leq g(u)}
.

Let
\displaystyle{H(z)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}h(u,z)du}
. Then
H
is holomorphic on
\mathbb{C}
and we have :

\displaystyle{\frac{\partial H}{\partial z}(z_{0})=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}h(u,z_{0})du}

(3.10)

\rhd
Let
\displaystyle{=h(u,z)=\mathcal{A}(z,u)f(u)=e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2}+\sqrt{2}uz-\frac{z^{2}}{2}}f(u)}
. We have already proven that
\mathcal{A}(z;u)f(u)
is integrable for all
z
in
\mathbb{C}
. Because
\mathcal{A}(z,u)\in L_{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
f(u)\in L_{2}(\mathbb{R})

\rhd
The second condition follows from the fact that the exponential function is holomorphic.

\rhd
By applying the following inequalities

\displaystyle{(\epsilon|\sqrt{2}z|-|u|)^{2}\geq 0\,\,\forall\,\,\epsilon>0}

and

\displaystyle{|\sqrt{2}zu|=|\sqrt{2}z||u|\leq\epsilon^{2}|z|^{2}+\frac{1}{2\epsilon^{2}}|u|^{2}\,\,\forall\,\,\epsilon>0}

we deduce that for
\displaystyle{\epsilon=\sqrt{2}}
we get

\displaystyle{|\sqrt{2}zu|\leq 2|z|^{2}+\frac{1}{4}|u|^{2}}

hence the following inequality

\displaystyle{|\mathcal{A}(z,u)f(u)|=|e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2}+\sqrt{2}uz-\frac{z^{2}}{2}}f(u)|=|e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2}}|.|e^{\sqrt{2}uz}|.|e^{-\frac{z^{2}}{2}}|.|f(u)|}

\displaystyle{\leq|e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2}}|.|e^{2|z|^{2}+\frac{1}{4}|u|^{2}}|.|e^{-\frac{z^{2}}{2}}|.|f(u)|}

\displaystyle{=|e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2}+\frac{1}{4}|u|^{2}}|.|e^{2|z|^{2}}|.|e^{-\frac{z^{2}}{2}}|.|f(u)|}

\displaystyle{\leq|e^{-\frac{1}{4}|u|^{2}}|.|e^{2|z|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}|z|^{2}}|.|f(u)|}

\displaystyle{=|e^{-\frac{1}{4}|u|^{2}}|.|e^{\frac{5}{2}|z|^{2}}|.|f(u)|}

\displaystyle{=|e^{\frac{5}{2}|z|^{2}-\frac{1}{4}|u|^{2}}|.|f(u)|}

Then we observe that the function
\displaystyle{g(u)=e^{\frac{5}{2}|z|^{2}-\frac{1}{4}|u|^{2}}|f(u)|}
is non-negative and integrable (because
\displaystyle{e^{\frac{5}{2}|z|^{2}-\frac{1}{4}|u|^{2}}\in L_{2}(\mathbb{R})\,\,\mbox{and}\,\,f(u)\in L_{2}(\mathbb{R})}
).

Therefore the third condition of above theorem is also satisfied and we conclude that

\displaystyle{SB[f](z)=\varphi(z)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{A}(z,u)f(u)du}
is a holomorphic function.

\rhd
Now, we will show that the Segal-Bargmann transform is injective, i.e. prove that
SB(f)=0
implies
f=0.

Let
\displaystyle{z=\frac{iy}{\sqrt{2}}}
then we have

\displaystyle{SB[f](i\frac{y}{\sqrt{2}})=e^{\frac{y^{2}}{4}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{\frac{-u^{2}}{2}}f(u)e^{iyu}du}

\displaystyle{=e^{\frac{y^{2}}{4}}\mathfrak{F}(e^{\frac{-u^{2}}{2}}f(u))(y)}
where
\mathfrak{F}
is the Fourier transform on
L_{1}(\mathbb{R})

The function
\displaystyle{e^{\frac{-u^{2}}{2}}f(u)}
is an element of
\displaystyle{L_{1}(\mathbb{R})\cap L_{2}(\mathbb{R})}
and the Fourier transform is a
bijective operator on
L_{2}(\mathbb{R})
. Hence
\displaystyle{SB[f](z)=0}
implies
\displaystyle{e^{\frac{-u^{2}}{2}}f(u)=0}
Consequently, we also have
f(u)=0
, which proves injectivity.
\blacksquare

\rhd
Frequently, we denote the Segal-Bargmann
SB
by
\mathcal{B}
called Bargmann transform.We denote its range by
SB(L_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
, on this space we define the following inner product:

\displaystyle{<\varphi,\psi>_{SB}=<f,g>_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R})}}

(3.11)

where

f
and
g
are chosen such that
SB(f)=\varphi
and
SB(g)=\psi
.

Then we have

Theorem 3.7
.

(
Unitarity of Segal-Bargmann transform
)

(i)
SB
is an unitary transform from
L_{2}(\mathbb{R})
to
SB(L_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
i.e. an isomorphism between
L_{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
SB(L_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
) that preserves the inner product..

(ii)
(SB(L_{2}(\mathbb{R})),<,>_{SB})
is a reproducing kernel Hilbert space.

(iii)
SB(L_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
and Bargmann space
\mathbb{B}
have same kernel.

(iv)
SB(L_{2}(\mathbb{R}))=\mathbb{B}
.

Proof

(i) The range
SB(L_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
of the Segal-Bargmann transform is a vector space.

As the Segal-Bargmann transform is injective, then the inner product defined by

\displaystyle{<\varphi,\psi>_{SB}=<f,g>_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R})}}

where
f
and
g
are chosen such that
SB(f)=\varphi
and
SB(g)=\psi
.

is well defined and it is clear that the Segal-Bargmann transform is an unitary mapping from
L_{2}(\mathbb{R})
to
SB(L_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
.

(ii) From (1.13) we deduce that, for
\varphi
in
SB(L_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
and
z\in\mathbb{C}
, we have

|\varphi(z)|\leq\pi^{\frac{1}{4}}e^{\frac{z\overline{z}}{2}}||\varphi||_{SB}

This implies that all evaluation maps are continuous. Hence
SB(L_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
is a reproducing kernel Hilbert space.

(iii) By the Riesz-representation , this implies that for each evaluation map there exists a
k_{z}
in
SB(L_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
such that, for all
\varphi
in
SB(L_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
,

\displaystyle{\varphi(z)=<\varphi,k_{z}>_{SB}}

and also by definition of the Segal-Bargmann transform,

\displaystyle{SB[f](z)=\varphi(z)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{A}(z,u)f(u)du}

\displaystyle{=<f,\overline{\mathcal{A}(z,.)}>_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R})}}

\displaystyle{=<SBf,SB\overline{\mathcal{A}(z,.)}>_{SB}}

\displaystyle{=<\varphi,SB\overline{\mathcal{A}(z,.)}>_{SB}}

for all
\varphi\in SB(L_{2}(\mathbb{R}))

Thus we find that

\displaystyle{K(z,w)=k_{w}(z)=SB(\overline{\mathcal{A}(w,.)}(z)}

\displaystyle{\quad\quad\quad=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{A}(z,u)\overline{\mathcal{A}(w,u)}du}

\displaystyle{=e^{z\overline{w}}\quad\quad\quad\quad}

where we have used (iii) of lemma 3.4.

(iii) The above kernel is equal to the kernel we found for
\mathbb{B}
in lemma 2.22 page 25.

Therefore,

(iv) We have by using theorem 2.25 page 27 that
SB(L_{2}(\mathbb{R})
is equal to Segal-Bargmann space
\mathbb{B}
.
\blacksquare

Proposition 3.8
.

One has

\displaystyle{(z+\frac{\partial}{\partial z})\mathcal{B}[f](z)=\sqrt{2}\mathcal{B}[uf(u)](z)}

(3.12)

\displaystyle{\sqrt{2}z\mathcal{B}[f](z)=\mathcal{B}[(u-\frac{\partial}{\partial u})f(u)](z)}

(3.13)

Proof

One has

(i)
\displaystyle{\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\mathcal{B}[f](z)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}A(z,u)f(u)du=\int_{\mathbb{R}}(-z+\sqrt{2}u)A(z,u)f(u)du}

\displaystyle{=-z\int_{\mathbb{R}}A(z,u)f(u)du+\sqrt{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}A(z,u)uf(u)du}

and

\displaystyle{z\mathcal{B}[f](z)=z\int_{\mathbb{R}}A(z,u)f(u)du}

Then

\displaystyle{(z+\frac{\partial}{\partial z})\mathcal{B}[f](z)=\sqrt{2}\mathcal{B}[uf(u)](z)}

(ii) Consider the rapidly decreasing function
\mathcal{A}(z,u)
of
u
with a parameter
z
arising in the definition of the Bargmann transform then we integrate by parts to get

\displaystyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}}A(z,u)[-\frac{\partial}{\partial u}f(u)]du={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[-A(z,u)f(u)]_{{}_{-\infty}}^{+\infty}}+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\partial}{\partial u}[A(z,u)]f(u)du}

\displaystyle{={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}+\int_{\mathbb{R}}(-u+\sqrt{2}z)A(z,u)f(u)du}

\displaystyle{=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}(A(z,u)uf(u)du}
+
\displaystyle{\sqrt{2}z\int_{\mathbb{R}}A(z,u)f(u)du}

This implies

\displaystyle{\sqrt{2}z\mathcal{B}[f](z)=\mathcal{B}[(u-\frac{\partial}{\partial u})f(u)](z)}
.
\blacksquare

Corollary 3.9
.

One has

(i)
\displaystyle{z\mathcal{B}[f](z)=\mathcal{B}[\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(u-\frac{\partial}{\partial u})f(u)](z)}

(ii)
