. Then it is readily verified that

DTD^{-1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccccc}a_{1}&\sqrt{b_{1}c_{1}}&&.&\\
\\
\sqrt{b_{1}c_{1}}&a_{2}&\sqrt{b_{2}c_{2}}&...,&..\\
\\
&\sqrt{b_{2}c_{2}}&...,&...&...\\
\\
...&...&...&..&\sqrt{b_{n-1}c_{n-1}}\\
\\
...&...&...&\sqrt{b_{n-1}c_{n-1}}&a_{n}\\
\end{array}\right)\\

(3.54)

Since
DTD^{-1}
is symmetric and
T
and
DTD^{-1}
have the same eigenvalues, this implies that the eigenvalues of
T
are real.

Suppose
\lambda
is an eigenvalue of
T
, then
T-\lambda I
is also of the form (3.53). If the first row and last column of
T-\lambda I
are deleted, then the resulting matrix is an
(n-1)\times(n-1)
upper triangular matrix with no zero entries on its main diagonal, since
b_{i}c_{i}>0
, for
i=1,2,...,n-1
. Hence this submatrix has rank
n-1
. It follows that
T-\lambda I
has rank at least
n-1
. However,
T-\lambda I
has rank at most
n-1
since
\lambda
is an eigenvalue of
T
. So by definition
\lambda
has geometric multiplicity one. This completes the proof,
\blacksquare

\bullet
Much work has been done to study the bounds of the eigenvalues of
real symmetric
matrices. Methods discovered in the mid-nineteenth century reduce the original matrix to a
tridiagonal matrix
whose eigenvalues are the
same
as those of the original matrix.

Exploiting this idea, Golub in
1962
determined
lower bounds
on tridiagonal matrices of the form:

S
=
\left(\begin{array}[]{cccccc}a_{1}&b_{1}&&.&\\
\\
b_{1}&a_{2}&b_{2}&...,&..\\
\\
&b_{2}&...,&...&...\\
\\
...&...&...&..&b_{n-1}\\
\\
...&...&...&b_{n-1}&a_{n}\\
\end{array}\right)

Proposition 3.42
.

(Golub
[17]
, Corollary 1.1)

Let
A
be an
n\times n
matrix with real entries
a_{ij}=a_{i}
for
i=j,a_{ij}=b_{m}
for
|i-j|=1
where
m=min(i;j)
, and
a_{ij}=0
otherwise. Then the interval
\displaystyle{[a_{k}-\sigma_{k},a_{k}+\sigma_{k}]}
where
\displaystyle{\sigma_{k}^{2}=b_{k}^{2}+b_{k-1}^{2}}

contains at least one eigenvalue.

Theorem 3.43
.

Every Jacobi matrix
S
of the following form:

S=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccccc}a_{1}&b_{1}&&.&\\
\\
b_{1}&a_{2}&b_{2}&...,&..\\
\\
&b_{2}&...,&...&...\\
\\
...&...&...&..&b_{n-1}\\
\\
...&...&...&b_{n-1}&a_{n}\\
\end{array}\right)

(3.55)

has
n
distinct real eigenvalues,
\displaystyle{\lambda_{1},......,\lambda_{k},.....,\lambda_{n}}
corresponding to eigenvectors
\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}^{1},......,\mathcal{U}^{k},.....,\mathcal{U}_{n}}
with
\displaystyle{||\mathcal{U}^{k}||=1}
and the first component
u_{1}^{k}>0
of eigenvector
\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}^{k}=(u_{1}^{k},.....,u_{j}^{k},.....u_{n}^{k})}
for all
k=1,....,n

In addition,
\gamma_{k}=u_{1}^{k}
, called
norming constants
, satisfy
\displaystyle{\sum_{k=1}^{n}\gamma_{k}^{2}=1}
.

Proof

Every eigenvector
\mathcal{U}^{k}
of
S
corresponding to an eigenvalue
\lambda_{k}
satisfies,

\displaystyle{a_{1}u_{1}^{k}+b_{1}u_{2}^{k}=\lambda_{k}u_{1}^{k}}

\displaystyle{b_{1}u_{1}^{k}+a_{2}u_{2}^{k}+b_{2}u_{3}^{k}=\lambda_{k}u_{2}^{k}}

……………………………………………

……………………………………………

……………………………………………

\displaystyle{b_{n-2}u_{n-2}^{k}+a_{n-1}u_{n-1}^{k}+b_{n-1}u_{n}^{k}=\lambda_{k}u_{n-1}^{k}}

\displaystyle{b_{n-1}u_{n-1}^{k}+a_{n}u_{n}^{k}=\lambda_{k}u_{n}^{k}}

equivalently

\noindent\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\displaystyle{b_{1}u_{2}^{k}=\lambda_{k}u_{1}^{k}-a_{1}u_{1}^{k}}\\
\\
\noindent\displaystyle{b_{2}u_{3}^{k}=\lambda_{k}u_{2}^{k}-b_{1}u_{1}^{k}-a_{2}u_{2}^{k}}\\
\\
...................................................\\
...................................................\\
...................................................;\\
\\
\noindent\displaystyle{b_{n-1}u_{n}^{k}=\lambda_{k}u_{n-1}^{k}-b_{n-2}u_{n-2}^{k}-a_{n-1}u_{n-1}^{k}}\\
\noindent\displaystyle{0=\lambda_{k}u_{n}^{k}-b_{n-1}u_{n-1}^{k}-a_{n}u_{n}^{k}}\\
\end{array}\right.\\

(3.56)

Suppose by contradiction that
u_{1}^{k}=0
then it follows from the first equation that
u_{2}^{k}=0
, then from the second that
u_{3}^{k}=0
, etc., hence
\mathcal{U}^{k}=0
which is a contradiction. Thus,
u_{1}^{k}\neq 0
for all
k=1,...,n
, so replacing, if necessary, the eigenvector
\mathcal{U}^{k}
by the eigenvector
\displaystyle{\frac{|u_{1}^{k}|\mathcal{U}^{k}}{u_{1}^{k}||\mathcal{U}^{k}||}}
we obtain
u_{1}^{k}>0
and
||\mathcal{U}^{k}||=1
.

Since the Jacobi matrix
S
is self-adjoint, its eigenvalues are all real. Suppose by contradiction that there are less than
n
distinct eigenvalues. Then some eigenvalue
\lambda_{k}
must have multiplicity more than
1
, so there are at least two linearly independent eigenvectors
\mathcal{U}^{k}
and
\mathcal{U}^{k}
corresponding to the eigenvalue
\lambda_{k}
. Then
\displaystyle{\mathcal{W}^{k}=v_{1}^{k}\mathcal{U}^{k}-u_{1}^{k}\mathcal{V}^{k}}
is also an eigenvector corresponding to the eigenvalue
\lambda_{k}
, but
w_{1}^{k}=0
which is impossible.

Since eigenvectors corresponding to different eigenvalues of any self-adjoint matrix are necessarily orthogonal, we have that the square matrix
\displaystyle{\mathbb{U}=[\mathcal{U}^{1},.....,\mathcal{U}^{k},.....,\mathcal{U}^{n}]}
has orthonormal columns and, hence, satisfies
\mathbb{U}\mathbb{U}^{*}=I
. iv)), that is, the rows of
\mathbb{U}
are also orthonormal so, in particular,
\displaystyle{\sum_{k=1}^{n}\gamma_{k}^{2}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}|u_{1}^{k}|^{2}=1}
.
\blacksquare

\bullet
We come back to Cauchy problem (3.46) associated to the matrix:

\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccccc}0&\omega_{1}&0&...&0&0\\
\\
\omega_{1}&0&\omega_{2}&...&0&0\\
\\
...&...&...&...&...&...\\
\\
0&0&0&...&0&\omega_{n-1}\\
\\
...&...&...&...&\omega_{n-1}&0\\
\end{array}\right)

(3.57)

where
\displaystyle{\omega_{n}=\sqrt{n}(n+1)}
with
n\longrightarrow+\infty
.

i.e.

\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccccc}0&\omega_{1}&0&...&0&0&...\\
\\
\omega_{1}&0&\omega_{2}&...&0&0&...\\
\\
...&...&...&...&...&...&...\\
\\
0&0&0&...&0&\omega_{n-1}&...\\
\\
...&...&...&...&\omega_{n-1}&0&...\\
\\
...&...&...&...&...&...&...\\
\end{array}\right)

(3.58)

\bullet
We consider the Jacobi operator acting on
\displaystyle{\tilde{a}=\{\tilde{a}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}}

\displaystyle{(\mathbb{J}\tilde{a})_{n}=\omega_{n-1}\tilde{a}_{n-1}+\omega_{n}\tilde{a}_{n+1};\,\omega_{n}=(n+1)\sqrt{n},n\geq 1}

(3.59)

\rhd\bullet
Let us consider a second-order difference equation

\displaystyle{\omega_{n-1}\tilde{a}_{n-1}+\omega_{n}\tilde{a}_{n+1}=z\tilde{a}_{n},n\geq 1}

(3.60)

associated with the operator
\mathbb{J}
.

Clearly, values
\tilde{a}_{1}
and
\tilde{a}_{2}
determine uniquely a solution
\tilde{a}_{n}
of equation (3.60). In particular, the solutions
P_{n}(z)
and
Q_{n}(z)
are distinguished by boundary conditions.

\displaystyle{P_{1}(z)=1},\quad\displaystyle{Q_{1}(z)=0}

(3.61)

and

\displaystyle{P_{1}(z)=\frac{z}{\omega_{1}}},\quad\displaystyle{Q_{1}(z)=\frac{1}{\omega_{1}}}

(3.62)

\bullet
We consider Jacobi operators
\mathbb{J}
acting in the space
\ell_{2}(\mathbb{N})
with the scalar product
\displaystyle{<\tilde{a},\tilde{b}>=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\tilde{a}_{n}\overline{\tilde{b}_{n}}}

Definition 3.44
.

For the analysis of linear second order difference equations, the Wronskian, also called the Casoratian see
[1]
play an important role. It defined as follows :

\displaystyle{W(\tilde{a},\tilde{b})(n)=\tilde{a}_{n}\overline{\tilde{b}_{n+1}}-\tilde{a}_{n+1}\overline{\tilde{b}_{n}}}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

and we define

\displaystyle{[\tilde{a},\tilde{b}]_{n}=\omega_{n}W(\tilde{a},\tilde{b})(n)}

It is clear that the wronskian is a bilinear skew-symmetric map. In addition we have

\displaystyle{W(\tilde{c}\tilde{a},\tilde{c}\tilde{b})(n)=\tilde{c}_{n}\tilde{c}_{n+1}{W(\tilde{a},\tilde{b})(n)}}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Moreover,
\displaystyle{W(\tilde{a},\tilde{b})=0}
when
\tilde{a}
and
\tilde{b}
are linearly dependent.

Then we verify easily that

\displaystyle{\sum_{k=1}^{n}(\mathbb{J}\tilde{a})_{n}\overline{\tilde{b}_{n}}-\tilde{a}_{n}(\mathbb{J}\overline{\tilde{b}})_{n}=-[\tilde{a},\tilde{b}]_{n}}

Let
\displaystyle{D_{max}=\{\tilde{a}\in\ell_{2}(\mathbb{N};\mathbb{J}\tilde{a}\in\ell_{2}(\mathbb{N}\}}
be the maximal domain of
\mathbb{J}
and we denote
\mathbb{J}
acting on
D_{max}
by
\mathbb{J}_{max}
.

By using the arsenal of Difference Operator Theory
[5]
Chapter VII] and the results of
[26]
or
[44]
on self-adjoint extensions of circle-boundary Jacobi matrices we deduce that

Lemma 3.45
.

If
\tilde{a}\in D_{max}
and
\tilde{b}\in D_{max}
then the limit of
[\tilde{a},\tilde{b}]_{k}
exists as
k\longrightarrow+\infty
. This limit is denoted
[\tilde{a},\tilde{b}]_{\infty}

Proof

Let
\tilde{a}\in D_{max}
and
\tilde{b}\in D_{max}
then
\mathbb{J}\tilde{a}\in\ell_{2}
and
\mathbb{J}\tilde{b}\in\ell_{2}
and it follows that
\displaystyle{(\mathbb{J}\tilde{a})_{j}\overline{\tilde{b}_{j}}}
and
\displaystyle{\tilde{a}_{j}\overline{(\mathbb{J}\tilde{b}_{j})}}
are in
\ell_{1}
so it follows that
\displaystyle{lim\,\sum_{j=1}^{k}[(\mathbb{J}\tilde{a})_{j}\overline{\tilde{b}_{j}}-\tilde{a}_{j}(\mathbb{J}\overline{\tilde{b}})_{j}]}
exists as
k\longrightarrow+\infty
.

Consequently

If we put
\displaystyle{lim\,[\tilde{a},\tilde{b}]_{k}=[\tilde{a},\tilde{b}]_{\infty}}
as
k\longrightarrow+\infty
then we get

\displaystyle{<\mathbb{J}_{max}\tilde{a},\tilde{b}>-<\tilde{a},\mathbb{J}_{max}\tilde{b}>=-[\tilde{a},\tilde{b}]_{\infty}}

(3.63)

\rhd
Let
\mathcal{D}_{0}
be the set of
\tilde{a}
in
\ell_{2}
having a non-zero finite number of components, then
\displaystyle{\mathbb{J}_{\mid\mathcal{D}_{0}}}
is a symmetric operator and it admits a closure that will be denoted by
\mathbb{J}_{min}=\mathbb{J}
of domain

\displaystyle{D_{min}=\{\tilde{a}\in\ell_{2};\exists\tilde{a}_{k}\in\mathcal{D}_{0},lim\,\tilde{a}_{k}=\tilde{a}\,and\,\exists\tilde{b}\in\ell_{2};lim\,(\mathbb{J}\tilde{a}_{k})=\tilde{b},k\longrightarrow+\infty\}}

(3.64)

\rhd
Let
\lambda
be a complex number with nonzero imaginary part,
\Im m\lambda\neq 0
. Assume that
\Im m\lambda>0
. By
\mathcal{R}_{\lambda}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\overline{\lambda}}
we denote the ranges of the operators
\mathbb{J}-\lambda I
and
\mathbb{J}-\overline{\lambda}I
, respectively, and by
\mathcal{N}_{\lambda}
and
\mathcal{N}_{\overline{\lambda}}
we denote their orthogonal complements in the space
\ell_{2}
.

The spaces
\mathcal{N}_{\lambda}
and
\mathcal{N}_{\overline{\lambda}}
are called the
defect subspaces
corresponding to the numbers
\lambda
and
\overline{\lambda}
. It is known that the dimensions
n_{+}=dim\mathcal{N}_{\lambda}
and
n_{-}=dim\mathcal{N}_{\overline{\lambda}}
are the same in the upper and lower half-planes. The numbers
n_{+}
and
n_{-}
are called
defect numbers
, and the pair
(n_{+},n_{-})
is the
defect index
of the operator
\mathbb{J}
. The operator
\mathbb{J}
is self-adjoint if and only if its defect numbers satisfy the equality
n_{+}=n_{-}=0
.

Using Theorem 1.5, Ch. VII,
[5]
we deduce the following properties

(
\bullet_{1}
)
\displaystyle{D_{min}=\{\tilde{a}\in D_{max};[\tilde{a},\tilde{b}]_{\infty}=0\,\quad\forall\,\psi\in D_{max}\}}
.

(
\bullet_{2}
)
\mathbb{J}_{min}
is a closed operator symmetric with indices of default (1, 1).

(
\bullet_{3}
)
\mathbb{J}_{max}
is the adjoint of
\mathbb{J}_{min}
.

(
\bullet_{4}
) If
u
and
v
are two independent solutions then at infinity, the minimal domain of
\mathbb{J}_{min}
is caracterized by

\displaystyle{D_{min}\{\tilde{a}\in D_{max};[\tilde{a},u]_{\infty}=[\tilde{a},u]_{\infty}=0\}}

Definition 3.46
.

(
limit circle at infinity
) [42]

The Jacobi operator is said to have a
limit circle at infinity
if a solution
\tilde{a}
of
\mathbb{J}\tilde{a}=z\tilde{a}
with
\tilde{a}_{1}=1
belongs to
\ell_{2}(\mathbb{N})
for some
z\in\mathbb{C}
.

Lemma 3.47
.

Consider
\mathbb{J}\tilde{a}=0
with
\tilde{a}_{1}=1
, then
\tilde{a}\in\ell_{2}(\mathbb{N})

Proof

\displaystyle{n\sqrt{n-1}\tilde{a}_{n-1}+(n+1)\sqrt{n}\tilde{a}_{n+1}=0,n\geq 2\iff\tilde{a}_{n+1}=-\frac{n\sqrt{n-1}}{(n+1)\sqrt{n}}\tilde{a}_{n-1},n\geq 2}

\displaystyle{\iff\tilde{a}_{n+1}=-\frac{\sqrt{n(n-1)}}{n+1}\tilde{a}_{n-1}}

We observe that
\tilde{a}_{2}=0
,
\tilde{a}_{4}=0
then for every even
n=2p;p\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
\tilde{a}_{n}=0
.

For every odd
n=2p+1;p\in\mathbb{N}
, we have :

\displaystyle{\tilde{a}_{2p+1}=-\frac{\sqrt{2p(2p-1)}}{2p+1}\tilde{a}_{2p-1};p\in\mathbb{N}}
. It follows that

\displaystyle{\tilde{a}_{2p+1}=(-1)^{p}\prod_{j=1}^{p}\frac{\sqrt{2j(2j-1)}}{2j+1};p\in\mathbb{N}}
.

\displaystyle{|\tilde{a}_{2p+1}|=\prod_{j=1}^{p}\frac{\sqrt{2j(2j-1)}}{2j+1};p\in\mathbb{N}}
.

\displaystyle{=exp\{ln[\prod_{j=1}^{p}\frac{\sqrt{2j(2j-1)}}{2j+1}]\}}

\displaystyle{=exp\{\sum_{j=1}^{p}[ln\sqrt{2j(2j-1)}-ln(2j+1)]\}}

\displaystyle{=exp\{\sum_{j=1}^{p}[\frac{1}{2}ln2j(2j-1)-ln(2j+1)]\}}

\displaystyle{=exp\{\sum_{j=1}^{p}[\frac{1}{2}ln2j+\frac{1}{2}ln(2j-1)-ln(2j+1)]\}}

\displaystyle{=exp\{\sum_{j=1}^{p}[\frac{1}{2}ln(1-\frac{1}{2j})-ln2j-ln(1+\frac{1}{2j})]\}}

\displaystyle{=exp\{\sum_{j=1}^{p}[\frac{1}{2}ln2j+\frac{1}{2}ln2j+\frac{1}{2}ln(1-\frac{1}{2j})-ln2j-ln(1+\frac{1}{2j})]\}}

\displaystyle{=exp\{\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{p}ln(1-\frac{1}{2j})-\sum_{j=1}^{p}ln(1+\frac{1}{2j})\}}

\displaystyle{=exp\{\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{p}-\frac{1}{2j}+\epsilon(\frac{1}{j})(\frac{1}{j})-\sum_{j=1}^{p}\frac{1}{2j}+\epsilon(\frac{1}{j})(\frac{1}{j})\}}

\displaystyle{=exp\{\sum_{j=1}^{p}-\frac{1}{4j}-\frac{1}{2j}+\epsilon(\frac{1}{j})(\frac{1}{j})\}}

\displaystyle{=exp\{\sum_{j=1}^{p}-\frac{3}{4j}+\epsilon(\frac{1}{j})(\frac{1}{j})\}}

\displaystyle{=exp\{-\frac{3}{4}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\frac{1}{j}+O(\frac{1}{j})\}}

Let
\displaystyle{S_{p}=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\frac{1}{j}}
then
\displaystyle{|\tilde{a}_{2p+1}|\sim\frac{1}{e^{\frac{3}{4}S_{p}}}}
;
p\longrightarrow+\infty
.

Lemma 3.48
.

\displaystyle{\frac{1}{e^{\frac{3}{4}S_{p}}}\leq\frac{1}{p^{\frac{3}{4}}}}

Proof

We begin by observing that:

\displaystyle{\frac{1}{e^{\frac{3}{4}S_{p}}}\leq\frac{1}{p^{\frac{3}{4}}}\iff}

\displaystyle{e^{\frac{3}{4}S_{p}}\geq p^{\frac{3}{4}}\iff}

\displaystyle{e^{\frac{3}{4}S_{p}}\geq e^{\frac{3}{4}}ln(p)}\iff

\displaystyle{S_{p}\geq ln(p)\iff}

\displaystyle{S_{p}-ln(p){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\geq}0}
.

Calculus students can approximate the integral
\displaystyle{\int_{1}^{p}\frac{dx}{x}=ln(p)}
by inscribed and circumscribed rectangles, and hence obtain the inequalities (for
p>1
)

\displaystyle{\frac{1}{p}<\sum_{j=1}^{p}\frac{1}{j}-ln(p)<1}

Better sub-estimations
\displaystyle{\frac{1}{2(p+1}+\gamma{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}<}S_{p}-ln(p){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}<}\frac{1}{2p}+\gamma}
(see
[47]
) where
\gamma
is Euler’s constant given by:

\displaystyle{\gamma=\lim\limits_{p\longrightarrow+\infty}(\sum_{j=1}^{p}-ln(p))}
. This limit is close to
0.5

Hence, there is
C>0
such that

\displaystyle{|\tilde{a}_{2p+1}\leq\frac{C}{p^{\frac{3}{4}}}\,\,\text{ in particular}|\tilde{a}_{2p+1}|^{2}\leq\frac{C^{2}}{p^{\frac{3}{2}}}}

(3.65)

so that
\displaystyle{|\tilde{a}\in\ell(\mathbb{N})}

\blacksquare

As a consequence of this Lemma, there exists a self-adjoint extension of the Jacobi operator
\mathbb{J}
subject to a boundary condition at
\infty
, so that the spectrum of
\mathbb{J}
in
\ell_{2}(\mathbb{N})
consists of a countable set of simple real isolated eigenvalues see
[39]
.

The existence of the self-adjoint extension of
\mathbb{J}
guarantees local well-posedness of the Cauchy problem (3.42)

Remark 3.49
.

(
CMP 1998
)

In an article in Communications in Mathematical Physics, 199, (1998),

we have presented a complete scattering analysis of the cubic Heun’s operator
\displaystyle{\mathbb{H}=A^{*}(A+A^{*})A}
acting on Bargmann space
\mathbb{B}
defined by:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\displaystyle{(\mathbb{H}\varphi)_{1}=\sqrt{2}\varphi_{2}}\\
\\
\displaystyle{(\mathbb{H}\varphi)_{n}=(n-1)\sqrt{n}\varphi_{n-1}+n\sqrt{n+1}\varphi_{n+1};\,\,n\geq 2}\\
\end{array}\right.\\

(3.66)

where

\displaystyle{\varphi(z)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\varphi_{n}e_{n}(z)}\text{and}\,\,\displaystyle{e_{n}(z)=\frac{z^{n}}{\sqrt{n!}}}

In particular, the boundary conditions at infinity are used in a description of all maximal dissipative extensions in Bargmann space of the minimal cubic Heun’s operator.The characteristic functions of the dissipative extensions are computed.Completeness theorems are obtained for the system of generalized eigenvectors associated to it.

This operator play an important role in Reggeon field theory.

4
Domination of
\displaystyle{\sum_{i+j<2k}a_{i,j}A^{*^{i}}A^{j}}
by
\displaystyle{A^{*^{k}}A^{k};\,a_{i,j}\in\mathbb{C}}

4.1
On the domination of
\displaystyle{\sum_{i+j<2k}a_{i,j}A^{*^{i}}A^{j}}
by
\displaystyle{A^{*^{k}}A^{k}}

Recalling some useful definitions and classical lemmas:

Lemma 4.1
.

(classical lemma)

Let
p,q\geq 1

\displaystyle{\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1.}
Then we have

(i)
\displaystyle{ab\leq\frac{1}{p}a^{p}+\frac{1}{q}b^{q}\quad\forall a\geq 0,\quad\forall b\geq 0.}

(ii)
\displaystyle{\forall\epsilon>0,\exists C_{\epsilon}>0;ab\leq\epsilon a^{p}+C_{\epsilon}b^{q}\quad\forall a\geq 0,\quad\forall b\geq 0.}

Definition 4.2
.

(
relative bounded between two operators
)

Let
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
are two operators acting on Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
with respective domain
D(T_{1})
and
D(T_{2})
.

We say that
T_{2}

dominated
by
T_{1}
(
T_{2}<<T_{1}
) or
T_{2}
is
relatively bounded
with respect
T_{1}
or (
T_{1}
-bounded) if,

(i)
D(T_{1})\subset D(T_{2})
.

(ii)
\exists\,a>0
and
b>0
such that
\mid\mid T_{2}\phi\mid\mid\leq a\mid\mid T_{1}\phi\mid\mid+b\mid\mid\phi\mid\mid\quad\forall\phi\in D(T_{1}).

Lemma 4.3
.

(
equivalence conditions

An equivalence condition of

\exists\,a>0\,\,\text{and}\,\,b>0\,\,\text{such that}\,\,\displaystyle{\mid\mid T_{2}\phi\mid\mid\leq a\mid\mid T_{1}\phi\mid\mid+b\mid\mid\phi\mid\mid\,\,\forall\phi\in D(T_{1}).}

(4.1)

is

\exists\,a^{\prime}>0\,\text{and}\,\,b^{\prime}>0;\,\,\displaystyle{\mid\mid T_{2}\phi\mid\mid^{2}\leq a^{\prime 2}\mid\mid T_{1}\phi\mid\mid^{2}+b^{\prime 2}\mid\mid\phi\mid\mid^{2}\,\,\forall\phi\in D(T_{1}).}

(4.2)

where the constants
a^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime}
are, of corse, in general different from
a
,
b
.

Proof

\bullet
It is easily seen that (4.2) implies (4.1) with
a=a^{\prime}
and
b=b^{\prime}
.
In fact:

As
\displaystyle{\mid\mid T_{2}\phi\mid\mid^{2}\leq a^{\prime 2}\mid\mid T_{1}\phi\mid\mid^{2}+b^{\prime 2}\mid\mid\phi\mid\mid^{2}}
then

\displaystyle{\mid\mid T_{2}\phi\mid\mid\leq\sqrt{a^{\prime 2}\mid\mid T_{1}\phi\mid\mid^{2}+b^{\prime 2}\mid\mid\phi\mid\mid^{2}}}

\displaystyle{\leq\sqrt{(a^{\prime}\mid\mid T_{1}\phi\mid\mid+b^{\prime}\mid\mid\phi\mid\mid)^{2}}}

\displaystyle{\leq a^{\prime}\mid\mid T_{1}\phi\mid\mid+b^{\prime}\mid\mid\phi\mid\mid}
.

\bullet
Whereas (4.1) implies (4.2) with
a^{\prime 2}=(1+\frac{1}{\epsilon})a^{2}
and
b^{\prime 2}=(1+\epsilon)b^{2}
with an arbitrary
\epsilon>0
.
In fact:

As
\displaystyle{\mid\mid T_{2}\phi\mid\mid\leq a\mid\mid T_{1}\phi\mid\mid+b\mid\mid\phi\mid\mid}
then

\displaystyle{\mid\mid T_{2}\phi\mid\mid^{2}\leq(a\mid\mid T_{1}\phi\mid\mid+b\mid\mid\phi\mid\mid)^{2}=a^{2}\mid\mid T_{1}\phi\mid\mid^{2}+b^{2}\mid\mid\phi\mid\mid^{2}+2\frac{a}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}||T_{1}\phi||\sqrt{\epsilon}b||\phi||}

and as
\displaystyle{2\frac{a}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}||T_{1}\phi||\sqrt{\epsilon}b||\phi||\leq\frac{a^{2}}{\epsilon}||T_{1}\phi||^{2}+\epsilon b^{2}||\phi||^{2}}
, we deduce that

\displaystyle{\mid\mid T_{2}\phi\mid\mid^{2}\leq(1+\frac{1}{\epsilon})a^{2}||T_{1}\phi||^{2}+(1+\epsilon)b^{2}||\phi||^{2}}
.
\blacksquare

Definition 4.4
.

(
T_{2}
is strictly dominated by
T_{1}
)

We say that
T_{2}
is
strictly dominated
by
T_{1}
(
T_{2}<<<T_{1}
) if,

(i)
D(T_{1})\subset D(T_{2})
.

(ii)
bis

\forall\epsilon>0\quad\exists C_{\epsilon}>0
such that
\mid\mid T_{2}\phi\mid\mid\,\,\leq\epsilon\mid\mid T_{1}\phi\mid\mid+\,C_{\epsilon}\mid\mid\phi\mid\mid\,\,\forall\phi\in D(T_{1}).

Definition 4.5
.

(
T_{2}
is
T_{1}
-compact
)

We say hat
T_{2}
is
T_{1}
-compact
if, for all sequence
\{\phi_{k}\}
in
D(T_{1})
such that the sequences

\{\phi_{k}\}
are
\{T_{1}\phi_{k}\}
are bounded and we can extract from
\{T_{2}\phi_{k}\}
a convergent subsequence.

Proposition 4.6
.

.

Let
T_{1}
be a closed operator on
\mathcal{H}
with
non-empty
resolvent set. Let
T_{2}
be an operator on
\mathcal{H}
. We say that
T_{2}
is
T_{1}
-compact (or relatively compact with respect to
T_{1}
) if
D(T_{1})\subset D(T_{2})
and one of the following equivalent assertions is satisfied.

(i) For all
\sigma\in\rho(T_{1})
, the operator
\displaystyle{T_{2}(T_{1}-\sigma I)^{-1}}
is compact.

(ii) For any sequence
(\varphi_{n})
bounded in
D(T_{1})
(i.e.
(\varphi_{n})
and
(T_{1}\varphi_{n})
are bounded in
\mathcal{H}
) then

(T_{2}\varphi_{n})
has a
convergent subsequence
.

Proof

(ii)\Longrightarrow(i)
Let
\sigma\in\rho(T_{1})
. Let
(\psi_{n})
be a bounded sequence in
\mathcal{H}
. Then
\displaystyle{((T_{1}-\sigma I)^{-1}\psi_{n})}
is bounded in
D(T_{1})
and hence
\displaystyle{(T_{2}(T_{1}-\sigma I)^{-1}\psi_{n})}
has a convergent subsequence in
\mathcal{H}
. This proves that
\displaystyle{T_{2}(T_{1}-\sigma I)^{-1}}
is compact.

(i)\Longrightarrow(ii)
Conversely, assume that
\displaystyle{T_{2}(T_{1}-\sigma I)^{-1}}
is compact for some
\displaystyle{\sigma\in\rho(T_{1})}
and consider
(\psi_{n})
bounded in
D(T_{1})
. Then
\displaystyle{(T_{1}-\sigma I)^{-1}\psi_{n}}
is bounded in
\mathcal{H}
. Then
\displaystyle{(T_{2}(T_{1}-\sigma I)^{-1}(T_{1}-\sigma I)\psi_{n})}
has a convergent subsequence in
\mathcal{H}
. This proves (ii).
\blacksquare

Proposition 4.7
.

Let
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
are two operators acting on Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
with respective domain
D(T_{1})
and
D(T_{2})
.

If
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
satisfy the following properties :

(i)
T_{2}
is strictly dominated by
T_{1}
.

(ii) The resolvent of
T_{1}
is compact.

Then

T_{2}
is
T_{1}
-compact.

Proof

Let
T_{2}
strictly dominated by
T_{1}
.i.e
D(T_{1})\subset D(T_{2})
and

\forall\epsilon>0\quad\exists C_{\epsilon}>0
such that
\mid\mid T_{2}\phi\mid\mid\,\,\leq\epsilon\mid\mid T_{1}\phi\mid\mid+\,C_{\epsilon}\mid\mid\phi\mid\mid\,\,\forall\phi\in D(T_{1}).

Then for any
\psi\in\mathcal{H}
,
\sigma\in\rho(T_{1})
we have

\displaystyle{\mid\mid T_{2}(T_{1}-\sigma I)^{-1}\psi\mid\mid\,\,\leq\epsilon\mid\mid T_{1}(T_{1}-\sigma I)^{-1}\psi\mid\mid+\,C_{\epsilon}\mid\mid(T_{1}-\sigma I)^{-1}\psi\mid\mid}

\displaystyle{\leq\epsilon\mid\mid\psi\mid\mid+\,\tilde{C}_{\epsilon,\mid\sigma\mid}\mid\mid(T_{1}-\sigma I)^{-1}\psi\mid\mid}

Let
\psi_{n}\in\mathcal{H}
and
||\psi_{n}||\leq M
. Since
\displaystyle{T_{1}-\sigma I)^{-1}}
is compact, then there exists a subsequence
\{\psi_{n_{k}}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
such that
\displaystyle{T_{1}-\sigma I)^{-1}\psi_{n_{k}}}
converges in
\mathcal{H}
. Then from

\displaystyle{\mid\mid T_{2}(T_{1}-\sigma I)^{-1}\psi_{n_{k}}-T_{2}(T_{1}-\sigma I)^{-1}\psi_{n_{m}}\mid\mid\leq\epsilon\mid\mid\psi_{n_{k}}-\psi_{n_{m}}\mid\mid+}

\displaystyle{\,\tilde{C}_{\epsilon,\mid\sigma\mid}\mid\mid(T_{1}-\sigma I)^{-1}\psi_{n_{k}}-(T_{1}-\sigma I)^{-1}\psi_{n_{m}}\mid\mid}

It follows that
\,\forall\epsilon>0
, , we can choose
N_{\epsilon}
such that
\forall\,\,k>N_{\epsilon}
and
\forall\,\,m>N_{\epsilon}
we have

\displaystyle{\mid\mid T_{2}(T_{1}-\sigma I)^{-1}\psi_{n_{k}}-T_{2}(T_{1}-\sigma I)^{-1}\psi_{n_{m}}\mid\mid\leq(2MC_{\epsilon,\mid\sigma\mid}+1)\epsilon}

So the subsequence
\displaystyle{(T_{2}(T_{1}-\sigma I)^{-1}\psi_{n_{k}})}
converges in
\mathcal{H}
and
\displaystyle{(T_{2}(T_{1}-\sigma I)^{-1}}
is compact i.e.
T_{2}
is
T_{1}
-compact.
\blacksquare

Definition 4.8
.

Let
T
be an operator acting on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
with dense domain
D(T)
.

We say that :

T
is accretive if
\Re e<T\phi,\phi>\,\,\geq 0\,\,\forall\phi\in D(T)
.

T
is
\beta_{0}
-accretive if
\exists\,\beta_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
T+\beta_{0}I
is accretive.

We will say that :

T
is maximal
\beta_{0}
-accretive if it does not exist an operator
\beta_{0}
-accretive which strictly extends
T
.

Theorem 4.9
.

Let
T
be an operator acting on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
with dense domain
D(T)
and
\beta_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
then the following properties are equivalent.

(i)
T
is maximal
\beta_{0}
-accretive.

(ii)
T^{*}
is maximal
\beta_{0}
-accretive.

(iii)
T
is
\beta_{0}
-accretive and for all
\displaystyle{\beta\in\mathbb{C};\Re e\beta>\beta_{0}}
, the operator
T+\beta I
applies
D(T)
on
\mathcal{H}
and we have :

\displaystyle{\mid\mid\phi\mid\mid\leq\frac{1}{\Re e\beta-\beta_{0}}\mid\mid(T+\beta I)\phi\mid\mid}

(iv)There exist a strongly continuous bounded semigroup
(e^{-tT})_{t\geq 0}
on
\mathcal{H}
with
-T
is its infinitesimal generator satisfy :

\displaystyle{\mid\mid e^{-tT}\phi\mid\mid\leq e^{\beta_{0}t}\mid\mid\phi\mid\mid\quad\forall t\geq 0,\phi\in\mathcal{H}}
.

Proof

This form of Hille-Yosida theorem and the above definition are given by Bardos appendix (C) of
[6]
.
\blacksquare

Theorem 4.10
.

(see: theorem 111 p.194 of book’s Kato
[22]
)

Let
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
two operators acting Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
with respective
D(T_{1})
et
D(T_{2})
such that
T_{2}
is
T_{1}
-compact.

Then

(i) If
T_{1}
is closable,
T=T_{1}+T_{2}
is also closable.

(ii) The respective closures of
T_{1}
and of
T
have same domain
D(T)=D(T_{1})

(iii)
T_{2}
is
T
-compact.

(iv)
T
is closable if
T_{1}
is closable.

In following, we begin to recall some fundamental inequalities.

Definition 4.11
.

Let
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
be linear operators in a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. We say that
T_{2}
is
p
-subordinate

(p\in[0,1])
to
T_{1}
if
D(T_{1})\subset D(T_{2})
. and there exists a strictly positive constant
C
such that

\displaystyle{\mid\mid T_{2}\phi\mid\mid\leq C\mid\mid T_{1}\phi\mid\mid^{p}\mid\mid\phi\mid\mid^{1-p}}\,\,\text{ for every}\,\,\phi\in D(T_{1}).

(4.3)

For
p=1
, we say that
T_{2}
is subordinate to
T_{1}
.

Definition 4.12
.

Let
T_{1}
be a linear operator in a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. The operator
T_{2}
is said to be
T_{1}
-compact
with
order
p\in[0,1]
if
D(T_{1})\subset D(T_{2})
and for any
\epsilon>0
, there exists a constant
C_{\epsilon}>0
such that

\displaystyle{\mid\mid T_{2}\phi\mid\mid\leq\epsilon\mid\mid T_{1}\phi\mid\mid^{p}\mid\mid\phi\mid\mid^{1-p}+C_{\epsilon}\mid\mid\phi\mid\mid}\,\,\text{ for every}\,\,\phi\in D(T_{1}).

(4.4)

The operator
T_{2}
is called
T_{1}
-compact
, if
T_{2}
is
T_{1}
-compact with
unit order
.

According to results on comparison operators established in
[27]
, then from the above definitions, we deduce the following classical results:

Corollary 4.13
.

Let
T_{1}
be an operator in
\mathcal{H}
with a
dense domain

D(T_{1})
. and at least one regular point
\sigma
.

We suppose that:

(
\alpha
)

D(T_{1})\subset D(T_{2})
.

(
\beta
)

\displaystyle{T_{2}(T_{1}-\sigma I)^{-1}}
is a compact. operator

Then for any
\epsilon>0
, there exists a constant
C_{\epsilon}>0
such that

\displaystyle{\mid\mid T_{2}\phi\mid\mid\leq\epsilon\mid\mid T_{1}\phi\mid\mid+C_{\epsilon}\mid\mid\phi\mid\mid}
for every
\phi\in D(T_{1})

Proposition 4.14
.

Let
T_{1}
be an operator in
\mathcal{H}
. We suppose there exists
p\in(0,1]
such that

\displaystyle{||(T_{1}-\sigma I)^{-1}||\leq\frac{C}{\mid\sigma\mid^{p}}},\,\,\sigma\in\Omega\,\,\quad|\sigma|\longrightarrow\infty

(4.5)

Where
\Omega
is an unbounded set of the plane complex.

Let
T_{2}
be an operator in
\mathcal{H}
such that
D(T_{1})\subset D(T_{2})
and

\forall\,\,\epsilon>0,\,\,\,\,\displaystyle{||T_{2}\phi||\leq\epsilon||T_{1}\phi||^{p}||\phi||^{1-p}+C_{\epsilon}||\phi||,\,\,\,\phi\in D(T_{1})}

(4.6)

Then

\displaystyle{||(T_{1}+T_{2}-\sigma I)^{-1}||\leq\frac{C}{\mid\sigma\mid^{p}}},\,\,\sigma\in\Omega\,\,\quad|\sigma|\longrightarrow\infty

(4.7)

Proof

For
\sigma\in\rho(T_{1})
, we write

\displaystyle{T_{1}(T_{1}-\sigma I)^{-1}=(T_{1}-\sigma I+\sigma I)(T_{1}-\sigma I)^{-1}=I+\sigma(T_{1}-\sigma I)^{-1}}

then for
\sigma\in\Omega,\quad|\sigma|\longrightarrow\infty
we have

\displaystyle{||T_{1}(T_{1}-\sigma I)^{-1}||\leq C|\sigma|^{1-p}}

(4.8)

For
\phi\in\mathcal{H}
we apply (4.6) to the element
(T_{1}-\sigma I)^{-1}\phi
to get:

\displaystyle{||T_{2}(T_{1}-\sigma I)^{-1}\phi||\leq\epsilon||T_{1}(T_{1}-\sigma I)^{-1}\phi||^{p}||(T_{1}-\sigma I)^{-1}\phi||^{1-p}+C_{\epsilon}||(T_{1}-\sigma I)^{-1}\phi||}

\displaystyle{\leq\frac{C\epsilon+C_{\epsilon}}{|\sigma|^{p}}||\phi||}
,
\sigma\in\Omega\,\,\quad|\sigma|\longrightarrow\infty

Hence,

\displaystyle{||T_{2}(T_{1}-\sigma I)^{-1}\phi||<1}

\sigma\in\Omega\,\,\quad|\sigma|\longrightarrow\infty

Then, by Newman identity:

\displaystyle{(T_{1}+T_{2}-\sigma I)^{-1}=(T_{1}-\sigma I)^{-1}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}[-T_{2}(T_{1}-\sigma I)^{-1}]^{k}}

(4.9)

We get:

\displaystyle{||(T_{1}+T_{2}-\sigma I)^{-1}||\leq C||(T_{1}-\sigma I)^{-1}||\leq\frac{C}{|\sigma|^{p}}}

\sigma\in\Omega\,\,\quad|\sigma|\longrightarrow\infty
.
\blacksquare

Corollary 4.15
.

From (4.9), if
\displaystyle{(T_{1}-\sigma I)^{-1}\in\mathfrak{C}_{p}}
then
\displaystyle{(T_{1}+T_{2}-\sigma I)^{-1}\in\mathfrak{C}_{p}}

Theorem 4.16
.

(
[23]
)

Let
T_{1}
be a normal operator with compact resolvent, and let

\displaystyle{T_{1}\phi=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sigma_{n}<\phi,e_{n}>e_{n}}

be its spectral decomposition. For
p>0
let

\displaystyle{T_{1}^{p}\phi=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sigma_{n}^{p}<\phi,e_{n}>e_{n},\quad D(T_{1}^{p})=\{\phi:\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}|\sigma_{n}|^{2p}|<\phi,e_{n}>|^{2}<\infty\}}

where
\displaystyle{\sigma^{p}=|\sigma|^{p}exp(ip.\text{arg}\,\sigma)}
, and
\text{arg}\,\sigma\in(-\pi,\pi]
.

(i) If
T_{2}
is subordinate to
T_{1}^{p}

(0<p<1)
, then it is
p
-subordinate to
T_{1}
.

(ii) If
T_{2}
is
p
-subordinate to
T_{1}

(0<p<1)
and
\notin\sigma(T_{1})
(
\sigma(T_{1})
is the spectrum of
T_{1}
), then
T_{2}
is subordinate to
T_{1}^{q}
for any
q>p
.

This implies that if
T_{2}
is
p
-subordinate
(0\leq p<1)
to a nomal operator
T_{1}
with compact resolvent, then
T_{2}
is compact relative to
T_{1}
.

Proof

(i) is the theorem 12.2 of the reference
[23]
and (ii) is the theorem 12.3 of the reference
[23]
.
\blacksquare

4.2
Some spectral properties of the Hamiltonian

\displaystyle{\mathbb{H}=A^{*^{k}}A^{k}+\sum_{i+j<2k}a_{i,j}A^{*^{i}}A^{j}}
on Bargmann space

Proposition 4.17
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
\displaystyle{P_{m}(A^{*},A)=\sum_{i+j\leq m}a_{i,j}A^{*^{i}}A^{j},\,\,a_{i,j}\in\mathbb{C};m\leq 2k-1}
with minimal domain:

\displaystyle{D_{min}:=D_{min}(P(A^{*},A))=\{\varphi\in\mathbb{B}\,\,\mbox{such that there exist}\,\,p_{n}\,\,\mbox{in}\,\,\mathcal{P}\,\,\mbox{(polynomials set) }}

\noindent\mbox{and}\,\,\displaystyle{\psi\in\mathbb{B}\,\,\mbox{with}\,\,p_{n}\longrightarrow\varphi\,\,\mbox{and}\,\,P(A^{*},A)p_{n}\underset{n\longrightarrow+\infty}{\longrightarrow}\psi\}}

(4.10)

then we have:

\displaystyle{\forall\,\,\epsilon>0,\exists C_{\epsilon}>0;\mid<\sum_{i+j\leq 2k}a_{i,j}A^{*^{i}}A^{j},\varphi,\varphi>\mid\leq\epsilon<A^{*k}A^{k}\varphi,\varphi>+C_{\epsilon}\mid\mid\varphi\mid\mid^{2}},

(4.11)

for all
\displaystyle{\varphi\in D(A^{2k})}
.

(ii) Let
k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
\displaystyle{H=S_{k}+P_{m}(A^{*},A);m\leq 2k-1}
then

\displaystyle{\exists C>0;\Re e<H\phi,\phi>\geq-C\mid\mid\phi\mid\mid^{2}\quad\forall\phi\in D_{min}(H)}
.

(iii)
\displaystyle{\exists\beta_{0}\in\mathbb{R};H^{min}+\beta_{0}I}
is invertible from
D_{min}(H)
to
\mathbb{B}
.

Proof

(i) This property is the corollary is 3.33 of the fundamental lemma.

(ii) is an immediate consequence of (i).

(iii) Let
\beta>0
, we consider
\displaystyle{H=S_{k}+P_{m}(A^{*},A);m\leq 2k-1}
then it follows that
\displaystyle{S_{k}+P_{m}(A^{*},A)+\beta I=[I+P_{m}(A^{*},A)(S_{k}+\beta I)^{-1}](S_{k}+\beta I)}
.

Now as
(S_{k}+\beta I)
is invertible then so just show that
\displaystyle{\mid\mid P_{m}(A^{*},A)(S_{k}+\beta I)^{-1}\mid\mid<1}
:

Let
\psi\in\mathbb{B}
, then we have :

\forall\epsilon>0,\exists C_{\epsilon}>0
;
\displaystyle{\mid\mid P_{m}(A^{*},A)(S_{k}+\beta I)^{-1}\psi\mid\mid\leq\epsilon\mid\mid S_{k}(S_{k}+\beta I)^{-1}\psi\mid\mid+}

\displaystyle{C_{\epsilon}\mid\mid(S_{k}+\beta I)^{-1}\psi\mid\mid}
.

\leq\epsilon\mid\mid(S_{k}+\beta I-\beta I)(S_{k}+\beta I)^{-1}\psi\mid\mid+C_{\epsilon}\mid\mid(S_{k}+\beta I)^{-1}\psi\mid\mid
.

\leq\epsilon\mid\mid\psi\mid\mid+(\epsilon\beta+C_{\epsilon})\mid\mid(S_{k}+\beta I)^{-1}\psi\mid\mid
.

As
\mid\mid(S_{k}+\beta I)^{-1}\mid\mid\leq\frac{1}{\beta}
then we deduce that

\mid\mid P_{m}(A^{*},A)((S_{k}+\beta I)^{-1}\psi\mid\mid\leq(2\epsilon+\frac{C_{\epsilon}}{\beta})\mid\mid\psi\mid\mid
.

in particular for
\epsilon<\frac{1}{2}
and
\beta>\frac{C_{\epsilon}}{1-2\epsilon}
we get

\mid\mid P_{m}(A^{*},A)(S_{k}+\beta I)^{-1}\mid\mid<1

\blacksquare

Proposition 4.18
.

[
P_{m}(A^{*},A)
is
strictly dominated
by
A^{*^{k}}A^{k}
]

(i)
\displaystyle{\forall\,\,\epsilon>0,\,\,\exists\,\,C_{\epsilon}>0;||P_{m}(A^{*},A)\phi||^{2}\leq\epsilon||S_{k}\phi||^{2}+C_{\epsilon}||\phi||^{2}}
. where
\displaystyle{m\leq 2k-1}
and
\displaystyle{S_{k}=A^{*^{k}}A^{k}}

(ii) If
m\leq 2k-1
,
k=1,2,...
then for every
\beta>0
,
\displaystyle{P_{m}(A^{*},A)(S_{k}+\beta I)^{-1}}
is compact.

(iii) For
m\leq 2k-1
,
k=1,2,...
, the resolvent of
\displaystyle{H=S_{k}+P_{m}(A^{*},A)}
is nuclear.

Proof

(i) Let
\displaystyle{\phi(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}\frac{z^{n}}{\sqrt{n!}}}
then we have :

\bullet_{1}

\displaystyle{||\phi||^{2}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}|a_{n}|^{2}}

\bullet_{2}

\displaystyle{||S_{k}\phi||^{2}=||A^{*^{k}}A^{k}\phi||^{2}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}n^{2k}|a_{n}|^{2}}

\bullet_{3}

\displaystyle{\exists\,\,C>0;||P_{m}(A^{*},A)\phi||^{2}\leq C\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}n^{m}|a_{n}|^{2}}
where
m=2k-1

Applying the property (ii) :

\displaystyle{\forall\epsilon>0,\exists C_{\epsilon}>0;ab\leq\epsilon a^{p}+C_{\epsilon}b^{q}\quad\forall a\geq 0,\quad\forall b\geq 0.}

of classical lemma to couple
(a,b)=(n^{m},1)
to obtain :

\displaystyle{\forall\epsilon>0,\exists C_{\epsilon}>0;n^{m}\leq\epsilon n^{mp}+C_{\epsilon}.}

i.e.

\displaystyle{\forall\epsilon>0,\exists C_{\epsilon}>0;n^{2k-1}\leq\epsilon n^{(2k-1)p}+C_{\epsilon}.}

If we choose
p
; such that
(2k-1)p=2k
it follows that
p=\frac{2k-1}{2k}
and
q=\frac{1}{2k}
satisfy
\displaystyle{\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1}
and
\displaystyle{n^{2k-1}\leq\epsilon n^{2k}+C_{\epsilon}}
. In particular :

\displaystyle{n^{2k-1}|a_{n}|^{2}\leq\epsilon n^{2k}|a_{n}|^{2}+C_{\epsilon}|a_{n}|^{2}}

and

\displaystyle{||P_{m}(A^{*},A)\phi||^{2}\leq\epsilon||S_{k}\phi||^{2}+C_{\epsilon}||\phi||^{2}}
.

(ii) As
P_{m}(A^{*},A)
is
strictly dominated
by
A^{*^{k}}A^{k}
then from proposition 4.7, we deduce that
P_{m}(A^{*},A)
is
A^{*^{k}}A^{k}
-compact and from proposition 4.6 it follows that for every
\beta>0

P_{m}(A^{*},A)
(A^{*^{k}}A^{k}+\beta I)^{-1}
is compact.

(iii) by Newman identity, we have:

\displaystyle{(H+\sigma I)^{-1}=(S_{k}+\sigma I)^{-1}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}[-P_{m}(A^{*},A)(S_{k}+\sigma I)^{-1}]^{j}}

(4.12)

Now, as
\displaystyle{(S_{k}+\sigma I)^{-1}\in\mathfrak{C}_{p}}
with
p>\frac{1}{k}
, it is nuclear if
k\geq 2
. It follows that the resolvent of
\displaystyle{H=S_{k}+P_{m}(A^{*},A)}
is nuclear for
k\geq 2
and if
k=1
the resolvent of
\displaystyle{H=S_{1}+P_{m}(A^{*},A)}
belongs to
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{C}_{1+\epsilon},\,\forall\,\,\epsilon>0}
.

Lemma 4.19
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
then
P_{k}(A^{*},A)
is
\frac{1}{2}
-subordinate to
S_{k}
. i.e. there exists a strictly positive constant
C
such that

\displaystyle{||P_{k}(A^{*},A)\phi||\leq C||S_{k}\phi||^{\frac{1}{2}}.||\phi||^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\,\text{for every}\,\,\phi\in D(S_{k})}

(4.13)

Proof

Let
\mathcal{N}=A^{*}A+I
and
B_{k}=A^{*^{k}}A^{k}
, then we have

\displaystyle{D(\mathcal{N}^{\frac{k}{2}})\subset D(P_{k}(A^{*},A))}
and
\displaystyle{||\mathcal{N}^{k}\phi||\sim||B_{k}\phi||}

As
\displaystyle{D(\mathcal{N}^{\frac{k}{2}})\subset D(P_{k}(A^{*},A))}
, we deduce that

\displaystyle{\exists\,C>0;||P_{k}(A^{*},A))\phi||\leq C||\mathcal{N}^{\frac{k}{2}}\phi||}
for every
\phi\in D(\mathcal{N}^{\frac{k}{2}})
.

Now, as
\displaystyle{||\mathcal{N}^{\frac{k}{2}}\phi||^{2}=<\mathcal{N}^{\frac{k}{2}}\phi,\mathcal{N}^{\frac{k}{2}}\phi>=<\mathcal{N}^{k}\phi,\phi>\leq||\mathcal{N}^{k}\phi||.||\phi||}
. It follows that

\displaystyle{||P_{k}(A^{*},A)\phi||\leq C||S_{k}\phi||^{\frac{1}{2}}.||\phi||^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\,\text{for every}\,\,\phi\in D(S_{k})}
.
\blacksquare

\displaystyle{\mathbb{H}=A^{*^{k}}A^{k}+\sum_{i+j<2k}a_{i,j}A^{*^{i}}A^{j}}

5
On regularized trace formula of order one of the Hamiltonian
\displaystyle{\mathbb{H}=A^{*^{k}}A^{k}+\sum_{i+j<2k}a_{i,j}A^{*^{i}}A^{j}}
on Bargmann space

5.1
Results of Sadovnichii and Podolski on regularized traces of abstract discrete operators

As it is known, the trace of a finite-dimensional matrix is the sum of all the eigenvalues. But in an infinite dimensional space, in general, ordinary differential operators do not have a finite trace.

In 1953, Gelfand and Levitan considered the Sturm-Liouville operator

\left\{\begin{array}[c]{l}-y^{\prime\prime}(x)+q(x)y(x)=\sigma y(x)\\
\\
y^{\prime}(0)=0,y^{\prime}(\pi)=0\\
\\
q(x)\in\mathcal{C}^{1}[0,\pi],\quad\displaystyle{\int_{0}^{\pi}q(x)dx}=0\\
\end{array}\right.\hfill{}(*)

and derived the formula

\displaystyle{\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(\sigma_{n}-\lambda_{n})=\frac{1}{4}(q(0)+q(\pi))}

\hfill{}(**)

where
\sigma_{n}
are the eigenvalues of the above operator and
\lambda_{n}=n^{2}
are the eigenvalues of the same operator with
q(x)=0
.

The proof of this regularized trace formula for the Sturm-Liouville operator can been found in
[14]

The same regularized trace formula for the same problem was obtained with different method by Dikii
[9]
.

For the scalar Sturm-Liouville problems, there is an enormous literature on estimates of large eigenvalues and regularized trace formulae which may often be computed explicitly in terms of the coefficients of operators and boundary conditions.

After these studies, several mathematicians were interested in developing regularized trace formulae for different differential operators. .

According Sadovnichii and Podolskii, these formulae gave rise to a large and very important theory, which started from the investigation of specific operators and further embraced the analysis of regularized traces of discrete operators in general form.

Among the results of Sadovnichii and Podolskii
[31]
established for abstract operators, we can recall that following :

Let
A_{0}
be a self-adjoint positive discrete operator of domain
D(A_{0})
acting in a Hilbert space, we denote by
\{\lambda_{n}\}
its eigenvalues arranged in ascending order,
\{\phi_{n}\}
is an orthonormal basis formed by the eigenvectors of
A_{0}
and
R_{0}(\sigma)
is its resolvent . By
B
we denote the perturbing operator, by
\{\sigma_{n}\}
we denote the eigenvalues of the operator
A_{0}+B
numbered in increasing order of their real parts, and
R(\sigma)
stands for its resolvent.

Also, assume that
BA_{0}^{-\delta}
,
\delta>0
is a compact operator belonging to some finite-order Schatten von Neumann class, i.e. the set of compact operators whose singular numbers form a convergent series
\displaystyle{\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}s_{n}^{p}}
for some
p>0
is traditionally denoted by
\mathcal{C}_{p}
.

For operators
A_{0}
and
B
in
[31]
, the following theorem is proved.

Theorem 5.1
.

(Sadovnichii-Podol’skii
[31]
)

Consider operator
A_{0}
and
B
be such that
A_{0}^{-1}\in C_{1}
and
D(A_{0})\subset D(B)
, and suppose that there exist
\delta\in[0,1)
such that the operator
BA_{0}^{-\delta}
can be continued to a bounded operator. Further, suppose that there exists
\omega\in[0,1)
such that
\omega+\delta<1
and
A_{0}^{-(1-\delta-\omega)}
is a trace class operator, i.e. in
\mathcal{C}_{1}
. Then, there exist a subsequence of natural numbers
\{n_{m}\}_{m=1}^{\infty}
and a subsequence of contours
\Gamma_{m}\subset\mathbb{C}
, that for
\omega\geq\frac{\delta}{l}
the following relation holds:

\lim\limits_{m\longrightarrow\infty}(\displaystyle{\sum_{j=1}^{n_{m}}(\sigma_{j}-\lambda_{j})+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\Gamma_{m}}\sum_{k=1}^{l}\frac{(-1)^{k-1}}{k}Tr((BR_{0}(\sigma))^{k}d\sigma)=0}

(5.1)

In particular, for
l=1
we have

\lim\limits_{m\longrightarrow\infty}\displaystyle{\sum_{j=1}^{n_{m}}(\sigma_{j}-\lambda_{j}-<B\phi_{j},\phi_{j}>)=0}

(5.2)

Remark 5.2
.

1) This theorem has been successfully applied to concrete ordinary differential operators as well as to partial differential operators, we can see some examples given in
[32

[33
).

2) We can found in
[34]
or in
[35]
, an excellent survey dedicated by Sadovnichii and Podolskii to the history of the state of the art in the theory of regularized traces of linear differential operators with discrete spectrum and a detailed list of publications related to the present aspect.

3) By applying above theorem of Sadovnichii-Podolskii, we have given in
[21]
the number of corrections sufficient for the existence of finite formula of the trace of concrete magic Gribov’s operator:

H_{\lambda^{\prime\prime},\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}=\lambda{{}^{\prime\prime}}A^{*3}A^{3}+\lambda{{}^{\prime}}A^{*2}A^{2}+\mu A^{*}A+i\lambda A^{*}(A+A^{*})A

(5.3)

where in Reggeon field theory, the real parameters
\lambda{{}^{\prime\prime}}
is the magic coupling of Pomeron,
\mu
is Pomeron intercept,
\lambda
is the triple coupling of Pomeron and
i^{2}=-1
.

We have obtained the following theorem:

Theorem 5.3
.

(
[21]
2016)

Let
\mathbb{B}
be the Bargmann space,
H=\lambda{{}^{\prime\prime}}G+H_{\mu,\lambda}
acting on
\mathbb{B}
where
G=A^{*3}A^{3}
and
H_{\mu,\lambda}=\mu A^{*}A+i\lambda A^{*}(A+A^{*})A
with
A
and
A^{*}
are the standard Bose annihilation and creation operators.

Then there exists an increasing sequence of radius
r_{m}
such that
r_{m}\rightarrow\infty
as
m\rightarrow\infty
and

\displaystyle{\lim\limits_{m\longrightarrow\infty}(\sum_{n=0}^{m}(\sigma_{n}-\lambda{{}^{\prime\prime}}\lambda_{n})+\frac{1}{2i\pi}\int_{\gamma_{m}}Tr[\sum_{k=1}^{4}\frac{(-1)^{k-1}}{k}[H_{\mu,\lambda}(\lambda{{}^{\prime\prime}}G-\sigma I)^{-1}]^{k}]d\sigma)=0}

(5.4)

Where

-
\sigma_{n}
are the eigenvalues of the operator
H=\lambda{{}^{\prime\prime}}G+H_{\mu,\lambda}

-
\lambda_{n}=n(n-1)(n-2)
are the eigenvalues of the operator
G

-
(\lambda{{}^{\prime\prime}}G-\sigma I)^{-1}
is the resolvent of the operator
\lambda{{}^{\prime\prime}}G

and

-
\gamma_{m}
is the circle of radius
r_{m}
centered at zero in complex plane.

5.2
The regularized trace formula for the Hamiltonian

\displaystyle{\mathbb{H}=A^{*^{k}}A^{k}+\sum_{i+j<2k}a_{i,j}A^{*^{i}}A^{j}}
on Bargmann space

Proposition 5.4
.

(i) For
m\leq 2k-3
, th resolvent of
\displaystyle{P_{k}(A^{*},A)(S_{k}+\sigma I)^{-1}}
is nuclear.

(ii) Let
\displaystyle{\phi=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}<\phi,e_{n}>e_{n}}
belonging to the domain
\displaystyle{D(S_{k})}
where
\displaystyle{S_{k}=A^{*^{k}}A^{k}}
,
k=1,2,...
and
\displaystyle{e_{n}=\frac{z^{n}}{\sqrt{n!}}}
. Then

\displaystyle{\exists M>0;||P_{m}(A^{*},A)\phi||\leq M||(S_{k}+I)^{p}\phi||}
where
m=2k-1
and
\displaystyle{p=\frac{m}{2k}=1-\frac{1}{2k}}
.

Proof

\bullet_{1}
For
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
S_{k}
is a discrete self-adjoint non-negative in Bargmann space
\mathbb{B}
operator with eigenvalues
\displaystyle{\lambda_{n,k}=n(n-1).........(n-(k-1))}
and corresponding eigenfunctions
\displaystyle{e_{n}(z)=\frac{z^{n}}{\sqrt{n!}},n\in\mathbb{N}}
.

\bullet_{2}

\displaystyle{\lim\limits_{n\longrightarrow+\infty}\frac{\lambda_{n,k}}{n^{k}}=1}

\lambda_{n,k}\sim n^{k}
(
n\geq k
).

\bullet_{3}
Let
\displaystyle{\Delta\lambda_{n,k}=\lambda_{n+1,k}-\lambda_{n,k}}
(
n\geq k
) then from lemma 3.37, we deduce that

\displaystyle{\Delta\lambda_{n,k}=k\lambda_{n,k-1}\sim n^{k-1}\sim(n+1)^{k-1}=[(n+1)^{k}]^{1-\frac{1}{k}}\sim\lambda_{n+1,k}^{q}}
where

\displaystyle{q=1-\frac{1}{k}\in(0,1)}
if and only if
k>1
.

\bullet_{4}
For all non-negative integers
i,j,n
, we recall that:

\star_{1}

\displaystyle{A^{*^{i}}e_{n}=\sqrt{\lambda_{n+i,i}}\,e_{n{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}+i}}}
(
n,i\in\mathbb{N}
).

\star_{2}

\displaystyle{A^{j}e_{n}=\sqrt{\lambda_{n,j}}\,e_{n{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}-j}}}
for (
0\leq j\leq n
). and
A^{j}e_{n}=0
if (
0\leq n<j
).

\star_{3}

\displaystyle{A^{*^{i}}A^{j}e_{n}=\alpha_{n,i,j}e_{n+i-j}}
if
i\in\mathbb{N}
and (
0\leq j\leq n
) and
\displaystyle{A^{*^{i}}A^{j}e_{n}=0}
if
0\leq n<j

where
\displaystyle{\alpha_{n,i,j}=\sqrt{\lambda_{n+i-j,i}\,\lambda_{n,j}}}
,
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
0\leq j\leq n
.

\rhd_{1}

\displaystyle{\alpha_{n,i,j}=\sqrt{\lambda_{n+i-j,i}\,\lambda_{n,j}}\sim\sqrt{(n+i+j)^{i}n^{j}}\sim n^{\frac{i+j}{2}}}
,
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
0\leq j\leq n
.

\rhd_{2}
The elements of matrix of
\displaystyle{P_{m}(A^{*},A)}
are
\displaystyle{O(n^{\frac{m}{2}})}
and the elements of matrix of
\displaystyle{(S_{k}+\sigma I)^{-1}}
are
\displaystyle{O(n^{-k})}
.

\rhd_{3}
The serie
\displaystyle{\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{n^{\frac{m}{2}}}{n^{k}}}

\displaystyle{=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n^{k-\frac{m}{2}}}}
is convergent if
\displaystyle{k-\frac{m}{2}>1}
i.e. if

\displaystyle{k>\frac{m}{2}+1}
. If
m\leq 2k-3
, this last inequality is verified and it follows that :

\displaystyle{P_{m}(A^{*},A)(S_{k}+\sigma I)^{-1}}
is nuclear.

As
\displaystyle{\alpha_{n,i,j}\sim n^{\frac{i+j}{2}}}
then
\displaystyle{\exists\,\,\beta_{i,j}>0,\gamma_{i,j}>0};\,\,\gamma_{i,i}n^{\frac{i+j}{2}}\leq\alpha_{i,j}\leq\beta_{i,j}n^{\frac{i+j}{2}}

In particular, there exist the positive constants
\beta_{i,j}
such that

\displaystyle{\alpha_{n,i,j}\leq\beta_{i,j}n^{\frac{i+j}{2}}},(j\leq n)

(5.5)

It follows that

\displaystyle{A^{*^{i}}A^{j}\phi=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}<\phi,e_{n}>e_{n}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{n,i,j}<\phi,e_{n}>e_{n{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}+i-j}}}

(5.6)

and

\displaystyle{||A^{*^{i}}A^{j}\phi||^{2}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}|\alpha_{n,i,j}|^{2}\,|<\phi,e_{n}>|^{2}\leq\beta_{i,j}^{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(n+1)^{i+j}|<\phi,e_{n}>|^{2}}

(5.7)

Now, as
\displaystyle{P_{m}(A^{*},A)\phi=\sum_{i+j\leq m}c_{i,j}A^{*^{i}}A^{j}\phi}
then

\displaystyle{||P_{m}(A^{*},A)\phi||\leq\sum_{i+j\leq m}|c_{i,j}|.||A^{*^{i}}A^{j}\phi||\leq\sum_{i+j\leq m}^{\infty}|c_{i,j}|\beta_{i,j}\sqrt{\sum_{n=j}^{\infty}(n+1)^{i+j}|<\phi,e_{n}>|^{2}}}

In particular

\displaystyle{||P_{m}(A^{*},A)\phi||\leq M\sqrt{\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(n+1)^{m}|<\phi,e_{n}>|^{2}}}\,;\,\,\displaystyle{M=\sum_{i+j\leq m}^{\infty}|c_{i,j}|\beta_{i,j}}

(5.8)

In the following we write this equation as follows:

\displaystyle{||P_{m}(A^{*},A)\phi||\leq M\sqrt{\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}[(n+1)^{k}]^{2\frac{m}{2k}}|<\phi,e_{n}>|^{2}}}\,;\,\,\displaystyle{M=\sum_{i+j\leq m}^{\infty}|c_{i,j}|\beta_{i,j}}

or

\displaystyle{||P_{m}(A^{*},A)\phi||\leq M\sqrt{\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}[(n+1)^{k}]^{2\delta}|<\phi,e_{n}>|^{2}}}\,;\,\,\displaystyle{M=\sum_{i+j\leq m}^{\infty}|c_{i,j}|\beta_{i,j}}

where
\displaystyle{\delta=\frac{m}{2k}}

AS
\displaystyle{(n+1)^{k}]\sim\lambda_{n+1,k}}
we deduce that

\displaystyle{\sqrt{\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}[(n+1)^{k}]^{2\delta}|<\phi,e_{n}>|^{2}}=\sqrt{\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\lambda_{n+1}^{2\delta}|<\phi,e_{n}>|^{2}}=||(S_{k}+I)^{\delta}\phi||}

where
\displaystyle{\delta=\frac{m}{2k}}
and
\displaystyle{m=2k-3}

In particular we have

\displaystyle{||P_{m}(A^{*},A)\phi||\leq M||(S_{k}+I)^{\delta}\phi||}

(5.9)

\blacksquare

Lemma 5.5
.

(
checking the assymptions of Sadovnichii -Podolski’s theorem
)

(i) Let
\displaystyle{S_{k}=A^{*^{k}}A^{k}}
and its eigenvalues are
\lambda_{n,k}\sim n^{k}
, then we have
\displaystyle{S_{k}^{-1}\in\mathfrak{C}_{1}}
If
k\geq 2
.

(ii) There exist
\delta=\frac{m}{2k}\in[0,1)
where
m=2k-3
such that the operator
\displaystyle{P_{m}(A^{*},A)(S_{k}+I)^{-\delta}}
can be continued to a bounded operator.

(iii) For
\displaystyle{\delta=\frac{m}{2k}=1-\frac{3}{2k}}
and
\displaystyle{\omega<\frac{1}{2k}}
, we have
\displaystyle{S_{k}^{-(1-\delta-\omega)}\in\mathfrak{C}_{1}}
with
\omega\in[0,1)
and
\omega+\delta<1
.

Proof

(i)
\displaystyle{\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n,k}}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n^{k}}<\infty}
if

k>1
.

(ii) From inequality (4.22) :

\displaystyle{\exists\,\delta=\frac{m}{2k}=1-\frac{3}{2k}\,\,\text{we have}\,\,||P_{m}(A^{*},A)\phi||\leq M||(S_{k}+I)^{\delta}\phi}
with
m=2k-3
and
k\geq 2

we deduce that

\displaystyle{||P_{m}(A^{*},A)(S_{k}+I)^{-\delta}\phi||\leq M||\phi||}
with
m=2k-3
and
k\geq 2
.

(iii)
\displaystyle{\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n,k}^{1-\delta-\omega}}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n^{k(1-\delta-\omega)}}<\infty}
if
\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}k(1-\delta-\omega)>1}\iff k(1-\frac{m}{2k}-\omega)>1}

\displaystyle{\iff k-\frac{m}{2}-k\omega>1\iff k-\frac{2k-3}{2}-k\omega>1\iff k-k+\frac{3}{2}-k\omega>1\iff}

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\omega<\frac{1}{2k}}}

and it easy to observe that
\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\delta+\omega}<1-\frac{3}{2k}+\frac{1}{2k}=1-\frac{1}{k}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}<1}}

\blacksquare

Remark 5.6
.

(i) To show there is a subsequence of natural numbers
\displaystyle{\{n_{s}\}_{s=1}^{\infty}}
and a subsequence of contours
\displaystyle{\Gamma_{s}\subset\mathbb{C}}
and to apply Sadovnichii -Podolski’s theorem recalled above to our operator, we choose
\displaystyle{l\geq\frac{\delta}{\omega}>\frac{m}{2k}2k=m=2k-3}
(
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}l\geq 2(k-1)}
) .

(ii) For
k=3
as in
[21]
, we obtain
\displaystyle{\delta=\frac{m}{2k}=\frac{2k-3}{2k}=\frac{3}{3}=\frac{1}{2}}
,
\displaystyle{\omega<\frac{1}{6}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}l\geq 2(k-1)=4}
.

Of above lemma and remark, the following theorem holds:

Theorem 5.7
.

(
\star\star\star\star\star
)

Let
\mathbb{B}
be the Bargmann space,
\displaystyle{\mathbb{H}=S_{k}+P_{m}(A,A^{*})}
acting on
\displaystyle{\mathbb{B}}
where
\displaystyle{S_{k}=A^{*^{k}}A^{k}}
and
\displaystyle{P_{m}(A,A^{*})=\sum_{i+j\leq m}a_{i,j}A^{*^{i}}A^{j}}
with
A
and
A^{*}
are the standard Bose annihilation and creation operators and
a_{i,j}\in\mathbb{C}
where
m\leq 2k-3
.

Then there exists an increasing sequence of radius
r_{m}
such that
r_{s}\rightarrow\infty
as
s\rightarrow\infty
and

\displaystyle{\lim\limits_{s\longrightarrow\infty}(\sum_{n=0}^{s}(\sigma_{n}-\lambda_{n,k})+\frac{1}{2i\pi}\int_{\gamma_{s}}Tr[\sum_{t=1}^{l}\frac{(-1)^{t-1}}{t}[P_{m}(A,A^{*})(S_{k}-\sigma I)^{-1}]^{t}]d\sigma)=0}

(5.10)

Where

-
\sigma_{n}
are the eigenvalues of the operator
\mathbb{H}=S_{k}+P_{m}(A,A^{*})

-
\displaystyle{\lambda_{n,k}=n(n-1)(n-2)......(n-(k-1))}
are the eigenvalues of the operator
\displaystyle{S_{k}=A^{*^{k}}A^{k}}

-
(S_{k}-\sigma I)^{-1}
is the resolvent of the operator
S_{k}

and

-
\gamma_{s}
is the circle of radius
r_{s}
centered at zero in complex plane.

Remark 5.8
.

It is useful to resume all the techniques of the proof of Sadovnichii-Podolskii’s

theorem to our operator as it was done in
[21]
with
k=3
for the magic operator

of Gribov.
